## THE CONFORMALLY INVARIANT METRIC ON CLE 4 I: SUBSEQUENTIAL LIMITS OF THE NON-SIMPLE CLE GRAPH METRIC

EMMANUEL KAMMERER, KONSTANTINOS KAVVADIAS, JASON MILLER, AND YI TIAN

Abstract. We consider the conformal loop ensemble (CLE) with the parameter κ = 4, the critical value at or below which the loops are simple and do not intersect each other or the domain boundary. We show that the loops of a CLE 4 uniquely determine a conformally invariant, local, and geodesic metric so that the metric ball growth from the domain boundary coincides with the uniform exploration of Werner and Wu. This metric was previously constructed in unpublished work of Sheffield, Watson, and Wu. Our approach differs in that we show that the metric arises as the renormalized limit of the graph metric on CLE κ loops as κ ↓ 4. In this first paper in a series of three, we prove that the subsequential limits exist and define a non-trivial conformally invariant metric on CLE 4 which is local and such that the metric ball growth from the boundary is given by the uniform exploration of Werner and Wu. In subsequent work, we will show that the subsequential limit exists as a true limit.

## 1. Introduction

1.1. Overview. The conformal loop ensembles ( CLE κ for κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 8) ) are random collections of non-crossing loops within simply connected domains in C [She09, SW12]. They are conformally invariant, meaning their laws are preserved under conformal automorphisms of the domain. CLEs are the natural loop variants of the Schramm-Loewner evolution (SLE) [Sch00]. SLEs and CLEs are conjectured (and in several cases rigorously shown) to describe the scaling limits of interfaces in critical statistical mechanics models on planar lattices [Smi01, LSW04, SS09, Smi10] as well as on random planar maps [She16, LSW24, GKMW18, KMSW19, GM21a, GM21c].

Just like for SLE, the value κ = 4 is critical for CLE. For κ ≤ 4, the loops are simple and do not intersect each other or the domain boundary. On the other hand, for κ ∈ (4 , 8) the loops are self-intersecting and intersect each other; each loop moreover intersects the domain boundary with positive probability [RS05]. In this paper, we focus on the critical case κ = 4. For κ ∈ (4 , 8), since the loops intersect, there is a natural metric that one can define on the loops of a CLE κ . It is simply the graph metric where the vertices in the graph are the loops of the CLE κ and there is an edge precisely when two distinct loops intersect. Since a CLE κ is conformally invariant and this metric is a function of a CLE κ , it is trivially conformally invariant. This construction does not make sense when κ = 4 since the loops do not intersect each other. The main aim of this paper is to show that nevertheless such a conformally invariant metric on the loops of a CLE 4 exists. See Figure 1 for numerical simulations of the metric.

<!-- image -->

The program of constructing the CLE 4 metric was initiated by Werner and Wu [WW13], who defined the distance from any loop in the CLE 4 to the domain boundary. Equivalently, they gave a definition of the growth of a metric ball from the domain boundary, where one explores the loops according to their distance from the domain boundary. They coined this the uniform exploration (cf. also [WW17, AS18]). The reason for this terminology is that as one grows the uniform exploration from the domain boundary toward a target point z ∈ D , the CLE 4 loops are discovered in a Poissonian way where, when a new loop is discovered, it is rooted at a point of the boundary of the component of the unexplored region containing z just before this time, chosen according to the harmonic measure seen from z ; equivalently, uniformly on ∂ D once that component is mapped conformally to D with z sent to 0. It was not proved in [WW13] that the uniform exploration corresponds to the metric ball growth from the boundary of a metric defined on the entire CLE 4 . The aim of this paper is to prove this conjecture and to show that the metric satisfies a number of important properties; we will spell these out just below in Section 1.2. It was also conjectured in [WW13] that the uniform exploration is a.s. determined by the CLE 4 . That this holds will be a consequence of the sequels [KKMT26a, KKMT26b] to the present paper. Let us already give a statement of the main result of this paper, which will be made more precise in Section 1.3.

Theorem 1.1 (See Theorem 1.3 for a more detailed statement) . For all κ ∈ (4 , 8) , consider a CLE κ in the unit disk D equipped with the graph distance D κ , where two distinct loops are adjacent if and only if they intersect. Then, for every sequence κ n ↓ 4 , there exists a sequence a κ n ↑ ∞ such that, along a subsequence,

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

for the product topology, where D is a distance on the set of loops of a CLE 4 in D , and where ℒ κ n ( x ) (resp. ℒ ( x ) ) is the loop of the CLE κ n (resp. CLE 4 ) that surrounds x . Moreover, the above convergence also holds for the distances to ∂ D , and the distances D ( ∂ D , ℒ ( x )) for x ∈ D ∩ Q 2 have the law of the times at which the uniform exploration discovers each loop ℒ ( x ) . Finally, the limiting metric D depends on the CLE 4 in a conformally invariant and local manner.

The reason that we have to introduce a normalization factor a - 1 κ in the statement of the theorem is that, as κ ↓ 4, the number of adjacent loops necessary to get from the boundary to the loop surrounding, e.g., the origin tends to ∞ . In fact, a - 1 κ is defined in Section 1.3 as the probability that the loop surrounding the origin touches the boundary; the number of loops in such a shortest path is then geometric (starting at 1) with success probability a - 1 κ , of mean a κ .

The uniqueness of the limit is established in the subsequent papers [KKMT26a, KKMT26b], so that the above scaling limit actually holds as κ ↓ 4, not only along subsequences. The metric that we construct in this article together with [KKMT26a, KKMT26b] was previously constructed by Sheffield, Watson, and Wu in unpublished work using an approach which is completely distinct from the one taken here. In particular, we will show that the metric arises as an appropriately renormalized limit as κ ↓ 4 of the adjacency graph metric of CLE κ loops as opposed to the indirect approach taken by Sheffield, Watson, and Wu.

Random planar map motivation. We anticipate that the CLE 4 metric will have applications in the study of random planar maps. Recall that a planar map is a graph together with an embedding into the plane so that no two edges cross. Two planar maps are considered to be equivalent if it is possible to deform one into the other using an orientation-preserving homeomorphism of the plane. In recent years, the limiting behavior of random planar maps has received a large amount of attention. Major results include the Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov convergence of rescaled quadrangulations toward the Brownian sphere [MM06, Mie13, LG13] and, for Boltzmann planar maps with large faces, the subsequential scaling limits obtained in [LGM11] together with the convergence toward the α - stable carpet (for α ∈ [3 / 2 , 2)) or gasket (for α ∈ (1 , 3 / 2)) established in [CMR25]. In this general framework, we expect that our random metric space describes the scaling limit of 3 / 2-stable maps. These are random planar maps where vertices of degree k ≥ 1 are assigned a weight of order k - 2 , first studied in the local limit in [BCM18]. Although these maps do not admit a scaling limit in the sense of Gromov-Hausdorff or Gromov-Prokhorov [Kam25b], one of the authors obtained in [Kam25a] a non-trivial scaling limit of the distances to the root, identified with a distance from the CLE 4 loops to the boundary introduced in [AHPS23], which will be shown in future work to agree with our metric. As shown in [Kam26] (and in [ADSH26] in the particular case of quadrangulations, see also [DHPW25] for the integrable fully-packed case on triangulations), the gasket of a critical O (2) loop-decorated planar map, obtained by forgetting the parts of the map that are inside the outermost loops, is an instance of the 3 / 2-stable map. This makes our expectation agree with the conjecture that the scaling limit of critical O (2) loop-decorated maps is described by the CLE 4 together with an independent critical Liouville quantum gravity (see, e.g., [HL24, Conjecture 2.1] for a precise conjecture).

As mentioned above, in the dual setting it has been shown in [CMR25] that the scaling limit of random maps with large faces, also called α -stable maps for α ∈ (1 , 2), with respect to the GromovHausdorff topology, is given by a random metric space known as the α -stable carpet (or gasket when α &lt; 3 / 2). Consequently, our metric space can be understood as the 'dual' of the 3 / 2-stable carpet. We also expect that it is possible to construct analogous duals for the (1 / 2 + 4 /κ )-stable carpets, where κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4), corresponding to a metric on the loops of a CLE κ , though they would be conformally covariant instead of conformally invariant.

Relationship to other work. In recent years, there have been many works aimed at constructing random metrics. In addition to the Brownian sphere [MM06, Mie13, LG13] and α -stable carpet and gasket [CMR25] mentioned above, these include the √ 8 / 3-Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) metric [MS20, MS21a, MS21b], the γ -LQG metric for γ ∈ (0 , 2) [DDDF20, GM21b], and the CLE metric in the simple [Mil21, MT25] and non-simple regimes [AMY25, MY25]. The present work together with its sequels [KKMT26a, KKMT26b] differs in several essential ways from these works primarily because the metric on CLE 4 is conformally invariant rather than covariant . In particular, arbitrarily small regions in the Euclidean sense can have arbitrarily large size in the CLE 4 -metric sense. This will manifest itself in particular in the proof of the uniqueness [KKMT26b], which we emphasize will not proceed by showing that different subsequential limits are bi-Lipschitz equivalent as in [GM21b, MT25, MY25]. We will instead obtain a representation of the CLE 4 metric in terms of the uniform exploration which we expect will have future applications in relating the CLE 4 metric to the scaling limit of 3 / 2-stable maps.

Next, we state the main results of the paper. The first main result states that there exists a weak geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling that can be obtained as a subsequential limit in law as κ ↓ 4 of the graph distance on Γ κ U (appropriately re-scaled).

Theorem 1.3 (Convergence of nonsimple CLE graph distance) . For any sequence κ n ↓ 4 , there exist a subsequence and a weak geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling D such that for every simply connected domain U ⊊ C , the random metrics a - 1 κ n D U Γ κn U converge in law along this subsequence to D U Γ U in R U Q × U Q equipped with the product topology.

Remark 1.4 . The above result actually holds in a stronger sense.

- Thanks to the uniqueness proved in the subsequent papers, the above result implies that a - 1 κ D U Γ κ U converges in law to D U Γ U as κ ↓ 4.
- Jointly with the above convergence, we also prove the convergence of a - 1 κ n D U Γ κn U ( ∂U, ℒ κ n ( x )) to D U Γ U ( ∂U, ℒ ( x )) for x ∈ U Q . See, e.g., Proposition 4.1 for more details on the convergence.

1.4. Tightness of random metric spaces. Let us give here some general background on subsequential limits of sequences of random metric spaces. In this subsection, we also explain why our approach differs from the classical approaches to random metrics.

When there is no natural embedding. A general approach for scaling limits of random metric spaces which are not equipped with a particular embedding in some reference metric space is to prove in some sense the convergence in distribution of the matrix of distances between random points toward the matrix of distances in the limiting metric space. This sense of convergence can be made precise using the so-called Gromov-Hausdorff, Gromov-Prokhorov, or Gromov-HausdorffProkhorov topologies. Proving the existence of subsequential limits in this context often requires precise concentration estimates and relies on chaining techniques [Tal05]. See for example the foundational work [Ald91] for convergence of random trees toward the Brownian continuum random tree (CRT). For random planar maps, the convergence of a process that encodes the metric space can be useful to obtain the tightness; see [LG07] for the existence of subsequential scaling limits of

2 p -angulations and [LGM11] for the analogous result for random maps with large faces. In our case, we do not work with such topologies but with the product topology where the loops are labeled by the points with rational coordinates that they surround. This is why we follow a different approach.

When there is a natural embedding in the plane. A case that is in some sense closer to this work is when the metric space has a natural embedding in a domain of the Euclidean plane. A famous example is the LQG metric; see [DDG23] for an introduction to this metric. When such an embedding exists, one can hope to compare the metric to the Euclidean metric and use some geometric arguments. For instance, in [DDDF20], the authors prove the tightness of an approximation scheme for the LQG metric using Russo-Seymour-Welsh arguments as well as independence across scales. To get the convergence, Ding, Dubédat, Dunlap, and Falconet [DDDF20] also rely on an Efron-Steintype argument which involves resampling the metric in some box. The uniqueness of the metric was then obtained in [GM21b, DG23], which characterize the limit using a list of axioms. The tightness of the chemical distance on the carpet of simple CLEs was obtained in [Mil21] and the limiting metric was later characterized in [MT25] by a list of axioms using closely related techniques. The same program was also achieved on non-simple CLEs in [AMY25, MY25] relying on a different resampling operation. In our case, in addition to the above-mentioned conformal invariance that complicates the analysis, the distance D U Γ U cannot be extended to a continuous function on U × U , so that one cannot hope for a tightness result with respect to uniform convergence, in contrast to [DDDF20].

The object of study of this paper is somewhat in-between these two broad classes of random metric spaces since, even though the CLE 4 is naturally embedded in a planar domain, the associated distance cannot be compared easily with the Euclidean distance. The fact that we take a different approach will be even more apparent in the next two papers [KKMT26a, KKMT26b].

- 1.5. Techniques and strategy of proof. Recall that the purpose of this paper is to construct a weak CLE 4 metric coupling D (see Definition 1.2) as the scaling limit, along a subsequence, of the renormalized graph distance a - 1 κ D U Γ κ U as κ ↓ 4.

In the literature, the construction of the limit of a sequence of random metric spaces is often split into two parts. The first one is establishing tightness, which by Prokhorov's theorem yields the existence of subsequential limits, and the second part consists in proving the uniqueness of such a limit. Tightness often requires careful work, depending on the topology, while the uniqueness part relies on a deeper understanding of the geometry of the limit.

We emphasize at the outset that proving tightness of the mapping from U Q × U Q to R ≥ 0 for the product topology is not the most difficult part of this paper. The main difficulty in the proof of Theorem 1.3 is to check that the subsequential limit indeed satisfies the axioms of Definition 1.2.

In order to construct D , we will first construct a related metric D ∂ , defined on

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

i.e., ∂U (the domain boundary) is treated as a distinguished point of the metric space. The metric D ∂ can be recovered from D via

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

We will nevertheless begin by constructing D ∂ rather than D , since its construction is simpler and because it will play a key role in the subsequent construction of D . The construction of D ∂ , as a subsequential scaling limit of the graph distance on Γ κ U ∪ { ∂ D } , will be achieved by first obtaining the scaling limit of the distances to the boundary using results of Aru, Holden, Powell, and Sun [AHPS23], who prove the convergence of the branching SLE κ ( κ - 6) exploration to the uniform exploration of the CLE 4 as well as the convergence of the loops of the CLE κ surrounding points with rational coordinates toward the loops of the CLE 4 . Then, we describe the metric balls from the loops using inversion invariance of the CLE (see [GMQ21] for κ &gt; 4 and [SWW17] for κ = 4).

Using the convergence to D ∂ and some tightness arguments, we construct D and the associated metric balls as a subsequential limit of the renormalized graph distance a - 1 κ D U Γ κ U on Γ κ U . The associated metric balls are actually constructed as subsequential scaling limits of metric balls on the CLE κ . We then prove that they are indeed given by the closure of the union of the domains surrounded by the loops at distance at most t for all t ≥ 0, thus matching the definition given in Section 1.2.

The conformal invariance of the subsequential limit (Axiom (III)) comes as a by-product of the conformal invariance of the CLE κ for κ &gt; 4. Moreover, Axiom (IV) will stem from the fact that the D -distance to the boundary is equal to the D ∂ -distance to the boundary, which will be given by the uniform exploration.

Thus, the only axioms which will require some work are the first two Axioms (I) and (II).

The first one, Axiom (I) (weak geodesic metric), will play a key role in the next paper [KKMT26a] to prove the existence of geodesics. It describes some properties of metric balls: namely their connectedness (I)(i) and the fact that metric balls are càdlàg with respect to the radius (I)(ii). The two points (I)(i) and (I)(ii), as well as (I)(iv) which describes in some weak sense the points in a geodesic between two loops, will be consequences of the subsequential limit and of the tightness of the balls for the Skorokhod topology. Let us stress that (I)(iii) is not a simple consequence of the triangle inequality due to the definition of the metric balls in Section 1.2. Surprisingly, (I)(iii) will require substantial work.

The second one, Axiom (II) (locality), is also difficult to check. At first glance, it seems to be a simple consequence of the restriction property for the CLE κ with κ &gt; 4. Yet, the Hausdorff convergence of the CLE κ loops surrounding rational points to the loops of the CLE 4 is not enough to get the convergence of the connected components of the set V ⋆ obtained by removing from V the closure of the union of the interiors of the loops which do not lie in V . Indeed, it may happen for instance that there is a large but very thin loop of the CLE κ .

One key intermediate result toward checking Axiom (II) is a uniform bound on the fatness of CLE κ loops when κ ↓ 4. We prove in Proposition 5.2 that for all ε &gt; 0, there exists δ &gt; 0 such that, uniformly as κ ↓ 4, with probability at least 1 - ε all CLE κ loops with diameter at least ε surround a Euclidean ball of radius δ . We also prove the same result when κ ↑ 4 in Proposition 5.3. Such a statement is clearly true for a fixed κ , and one can show that it is true locally uniformly in κ ∈ (4 , 8) using the space-filling SLE. However, when κ ↓ 4, the space-filling SLE degenerates. We prove Proposition 5.2 using couplings from [MSW17] and using the analogous statement for κ ↑ 4 and the convergence of CLE κ loops as κ ↑ 4 in terms of closed paths. The analogous statement for κ ↑ 4 is obtained via the coupling with Brownian loop soups.

Let us conclude this subsection with the following observation. One can note that, by conformal invariance, it suffices to consider the case where U = D . We record here the following equivalent formulation of the axioms of Definition 1.2, which will be more convenient to use in this paper. We chose to first present Definition 1.2 before the following axioms inasmuch as Definition 1.2 will be used in the further work [KKMT26a].

The same is true with a deterministic connected arc (resp. two deterministic and disjoint connected arcs) of ∂ D in place of ℒ 1 (resp. ℒ 1 and ℒ 2 ).
- (II) (Locality) Let V ⊆ D be a deterministic simply connected subdomain. Let { V j } j be the connected components of V ⋆ (cf. (1.1) ). Let I be a deterministic connected arc of ∂ D . For each j , let ϕ j be a conformal mapping from V j onto D that is a.s. determined by
- (1.3) { ℒ ∈ Γ : ℒ ̸⊆ V } and { ℬ t ( ℒ ) : ℒ ∈ Γ , ℒ ̸⊆ V, t ∈ [0 , D ( ℒ , ∂V ⋆ )] } ,

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

There exists a coupling of (Γ , D ) with some ( ϕ j (Γ | V j ) , D j ) 's such that

- Conditionally on (1.3) , the ( ϕ j (Γ | V j ) , D j ) 's are independent and their conditional laws are those of (Γ , D ) .
- For all j , for all ℒ 1 , ℒ 2 ∈ Γ | V j , we have D ( ℒ 1 , ℒ 2 ) ≤ D j ( ϕ j ( ℒ 1 ) , ϕ j ( ℒ 2 )) .
- For all j , for all ℒ ∈ Γ | V j , t ∈ [0 , D ( ℒ , ∂V j )] , the closure of the image under ϕ - 1 j of the intersection with D of the metric ball of radius t for D j centered at ϕ j ( ℒ ) is ℬ t ( ℒ ) .
- (III) (Conformal invariance) Let ϕ : D → D be a deterministic conformal automorphism of D . Then (Γ , D ) and ( ϕ (Γ) , ϕ ∗ ( D )) have the same law, where ϕ ∗ ( D ) is defined by setting ϕ ∗ ( D )( ϕ ( ℒ 1 ) , ϕ ( ℒ 2 )) = D ( ℒ 1 , ℒ 2 ) .
- (IV) (Uniform exploration) The collection { ( ℒ , D ( ℒ , ∂ D )) } ℒ ∈ Γ has the law of a uniform exploration of Γ (see Section 2.5 for the definition of the uniform CLE 4 exploration).

Proof. Let U be a simply connected domain of C which is different from C . One can define the coupling (Γ U , D U Γ U ) from the above coupling (Γ , D ) by taking the image under a conformal map φ from D to U . Such a coupling does not depend on the choice of φ by conformal invariance. Then, the family of couplings { (Γ U , D U Γ U ) } U satisfies the axioms of Definition 1.2. Conversely, such a family is determined by its restriction to U = D , so the two constructions are inverse to each other, and it suffices to check that each axiom of Definition 1.2 for all U is equivalent to its counterpart below for U = D . This is immediate for (III), (IV) and the geodesic axioms, whose statements involve only the loops, the metric balls and the conformally invariant notions of connectedness and of surrounding. For locality, φ maps the loops not contained in V to those not contained in φ ( V ), hence V ⋆ and its components to φ ( V ) ⋆ and its components, and the metric balls of D to those of D U Γ U . □

1.6. Outline. Let us give an outline of the content of the rest of the paper.

Section 2: Preliminaries. We will review the necessary background material on the Gaussian free field (GFF), Schramm-Loewner evolutions (SLE) with force points, conformal loop ensembles (CLE); couplings between the GFF, CLE 4 , and boundary conformal loop ensembles (BCLE); the uniform exploration; and Carathéodory convergence.

Section 3: When the boundary is a point. We construct the subsequential scaling limit of the CLE κ graph distance when κ ↓ 4 in the case where we identify the boundary of the domain with a point of the metric space. Building on results of [AHPS23], we prove that balls from the boundary correspond to the uniform exploration. Using the inversion invariance of the CLE in the annulus, we also describe metric balls from any loop in terms of the uniform exploration. This construction is useful in order to construct the metric in the case where the boundary is not identified with a point.

Section 4: When the boundary is not a point. In this section, we construct the subsequential limiting metric in the case that the boundary of the domain is not a single point; this is in fact the case in which we are primarily interested. We first show that the rescaled graph distances a - 1 κ D U Γ κ U for κ ∈ (4 , 8) are tight as κ ↓ 4. Then, we construct the associated metric balls and prove that they are càdlàg via a tightness argument and a comparison between these metric balls and the metric balls obtained in the previous section.

Section 5: Large non-simple CLE loops are fat. Next, in order to check that our metric satisfies the axioms of Definition 1.2, we establish a stronger convergence of loops than the Hausdorff convergence obtained in [AHPS23]. More precisely, we prove that the closure of the union of loops intersecting a given connected compact set converges. This is a consequence of the fact that uniformly in κ ↓ 4, large CLE κ loops surround a large Euclidean ball. To prove this fact, we will first prove an analogous statement when κ ↑ 4 using the coupling with Brownian loop soups and prove the convergence of simple loops as κ ↑ 4 in terms of closed paths. Then, relying on the couplings from [MSW17], we show the desired result for large non-simple loops. On a first reading, one can skip the proofs in this section. They are, however, of independent interest and may have other applications in further works on CLE.

Section 6: On the distance between two segments. In this section, we prove that for all ε &gt; 0 one can find two disjoint segments on the boundary of the domain whose CLE κ graph distance is at most ε a κ with probability at least 1 - ε , uniformly in κ ↓ 4. This technical result will be useful to check the axioms.

Section 7: Checking the axioms. We finally check that our subsequential limiting metric satisfies the axioms of Definition 1.2, concluding the proof of Theorem 1.3.

## 2. Preliminaries

2.1. Gaussian free fields. We will now briefly recall some of the basics of the Gaussian free field (GFF); see [She07] for a more in-depth review. Let G ⊆ C be a simply connected domain with harmonically non-trivial boundary, and let H 1 0 ( G ) denote the Hilbert space closure of C ∞ 0 ( G ) with respect to the Dirichlet inner product

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

The zero-boundary Gaussian free field (GFF) h is the random distribution defined by (the series converging in the sense explained below)

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where ( ϕ n ) n ≥ 1 is a ( · , · ) ∇ -orthonormal basis of H 1 0 ( G ) and ( α n ) n ≥ 1 is a sequence of independent N (0 , 1)-distributed random variables; the law of h does not depend on the choice of ( ϕ n ) n ≥ 1 .

A GFF on G is not a function but rather a random variable in the space of distributions on G , since the series in (2.1) does not converge in H 1 0 ( G ) a.s. Nevertheless, it converges a.s. in H - 1 ( G ), the dual space of H 1 0 ( G ), when G is bounded, and in the space of distributions on G in general (by conformal invariance). We say that a GFF on G has boundary conditions given by a bounded measurable function f defined on ∂G if it can be expressed as the sum of a zero-boundary GFF on G plus the harmonic extension of f from ∂G to G .

The following are two important properties of the GFF that we will repeatedly use.

- The Markov property. If h is a zero-boundary GFF on G and U ⊆ G is open, then we can write h = h 0 U + h U , where h 0 U is a zero-boundary GFF on U and h U is a distribution on G which is harmonic on U . Moreover, h 0 U and h U are independent.
- Conformal invariance. If ϕ : G → G ′ is a conformal transformation and h is a GFF on G , then h ◦ ϕ - 1 is a GFF on G ′ .

There is also a variant of the Markov property where one thinks of conditioning the values of the GFF on a set which is random, in analogy with the strong Markov property and stopping times for Brownian motion. Specifically, a random closed set A ⊆ G coupled with h is said to be local for h [SS13] if we can write h = h 0 A + h A where h A is a distribution on G which is harmonic on G \ A and, given the σ -algebra generated by ( A,h A ), h 0 A has the law of a GFF on G \ A with zero boundary conditions.

- 2.2. Chordal SLE κ ( ρ ) processes. The chordal SLE κ ( ρ ) processes are variants of SLE κ where one keeps track of extra marked points [LSW03]. To define them, we fix κ &gt; 0, let x L = ( x ℓ,L , . . . , x 1 ,L ) and x R = ( x 1 ,R , . . . , x r,R ), where x ℓ,L &lt; · · · &lt; x 1 ,L ≤ 0 ≤ x 1 ,R &lt; · · · &lt; x r,R , and let ρ L = ( ρ 1 ,L , . . . , ρ ℓ,L ) and ρ R = ( ρ 1 ,R , . . . , ρ r,R ), where ρ j,q ∈ R for q ∈ { L, R } and j ∈ [1 , N q ] Z with N L = ℓ , N R = r , where [ a, b ] Z def = [ a, b ] ∩ Z . Let ( g t ) denote the solution to the ODE:

for a standard Brownian motion B .

For any value of ρ L and ρ R , it is clear that (2.3) has a unique strong solution (when x 1 ,L &lt; 0 &lt; x 1 ,R ) until the first time t such that W t = V j,q t for some q ∈ { L, R } and j ∈ [1 , N q ] Z . Actually, it was shown in [MS16a, WW17] that, when ∑ k j =1 ρ j,L &gt; - 2 for all k ∈ [1 , ℓ ] Z and ∑ k j =1 ρ j,R &gt; - 2 for all k ∈ [1 , r ] Z , there exists a solution to (2.3) defined for all times t ≥ 0 (also for force points at 0 - , 0 + ), and for this solution the set of t ≥ 0 for which W t = V j,q t for some q ∈ { L, R } and j ∈ [1 , N q ] Z a.s. has zero Lebesgue measure. The uniqueness in law of such a solution is also shown in [MS16a]. Moreover, it was shown in [MS16a, WW17] that there a.s. exists a continuous curve η such that the domain H t of g t is given by the unbounded connected component of H \ η ([0 , t ]), for all t ≥ 0. The curve η is called the chordal SLE κ ( ρ ) in H from 0 to ∞ . (These processes are defined below for ρ ≤ - 2, for which the above condition fails; see [MSW17, MS19] for their continuity.)

An SLE κ ( ρ ) process in a simply connected domain G ⊊ C is defined as the image of an SLE κ ( ρ ) on H under a conformal transformation mapping H onto G which sends the starting point 0 to the desired starting point on ∂G and the target point ∞ to the desired target point on ∂G (the law of the image does not depend on the choice of the map, by the scale invariance of SLE κ ( ρ )). The force points on ∂G are then defined as the images of the corresponding force points on ∂ H .

One can also define a variant of SLE κ ( ρ ) in which the side of the marked point is chosen afresh for each excursion by an independent (possibly biased) coin toss. In particular, we fix κ &gt; 0 , ρ ∈ ( - 2 - ( κ/ 2) , ( κ/ 2) - 2) \ {- 2 } , and β ∈ [ - 1 , 1], and let X be a Bessel process starting from 0 with dimension

In both cases, we define the SLE β κ ( ρ ) process in H from 0 to ∞ as the collection of hulls generated by W via the Loewner equation (2.2). It was shown in [MSW17, Theorem 10.3] that SLE β κ ( ρ ) processes are a.s. generated by continuous curves for all κ ∈ (2 , 4) , β ∈ [ - 1 , 1], and all ρ ∈ ( - 2 - ( κ/ 2) , ( κ/ 2) - 4). Moreover, it was shown in [MSW17, Theorem 10.2] that SLE β κ ′ ( ρ ′ ) processes are a.s. generated by continuous curves for all κ ′ &gt; 4 , β ∈ [ - 1 , 1], and all ρ ′ ∈ ( - 2 - ( κ ′ / 2) , ( κ ′ / 2) - 2) \ {- 2 } .

For a general simply connected domain G ⊊ C , we define an SLE β κ ( ρ ) process in G between two distinct marked points x, y ∈ ∂G to be given by the image of an SLE β κ ( ρ ) in H from 0 to ∞ under a conformal transformation mapping H onto G which maps 0 to x and ∞ to y .

- 2.3. Conformal loop ensembles. Now, we will briefly review CLE and refer the reader to [SW12, MSW17, She09] for more details.

A CLE in D is a random countable collection Γ of non-nested loops ( γ j ) j ∈ J in D satisfying the following properties.

- (i) Conformal invariance : For any Möbius transformation ϕ : D → D , the laws of Γ and ϕ (Γ) are the same. This makes it possible to define CLE in any proper simply connected domain G ⊆ C as the image of a CLE in D under a conformal transformation mapping D onto G .
- (ii) Domain Markov property : For any simply connected domain U ⊆ D , we let U ⋆ denote the set obtained by removing from U the closure of the union of the interiors of the loops in Γ not contained in U . Then, conditionally on the loops of Γ not contained in U (which determine U ⋆ ), the restrictions of Γ to the components V of U ⋆ are independent, and each has the law of Γ conformally mapped to V .

It was shown in [SW12, She09] that for each κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 8) there exists a CLE κ where the loops can be constructed using a branching variant of SLE κ (specifically, a tree built out of a collection of SLE κ ( κ - 6) processes; we will describe this construction in more detail in Section 3.3.1). The loops in the CLE κ are then SLE κ -type curves. From the perspective of the branching tree construction, it is not obvious that the loops of the CLE κ do not depend on the choice of root, are continuous curves, and are locally finite. In the case that κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4], this was proved in [SW12] as a consequence of the representation of the CLE κ loops as the outer boundaries of clusters of the Brownian loop soup; see also [MSW17] for another argument. In the case that κ ∈ (4 , 8), this was proved as a consequence of the continuity of SLE κ ( κ - 6) [MS16a], the reversibility of SLE κ [MS16b], and the continuity of space-filling SLE κ [MS17].

If κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4], we have that CLE κ consists of simple loops which do not intersect the domain boundary or each other a.s. However, if κ ∈ (4 , 8), we have that the loops in the CLE κ are nonsimple and can intersect each other and the domain boundary. In the present work, we will focus on the κ = 4 case, for which the loops do not intersect each other or the domain boundary but nearly do. It was also shown in [SW12] that if one has an ensemble of loops which satisfies (i) and (ii), together with the further assumptions that the loops are simple and do not intersect the boundary or each other, and that the ensemble is locally finite, then it must be a CLE κ for some κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4].

- 2.4. Level lines of the GFF. Let λ = π/ 2 and let h be an instance of the GFF on H with boundary conditions given by - λ (resp. λ ) on R - (resp. R + ). Then, it was shown in [SS13] that a zero level line η of h from 0 to ∞ can be rigorously defined and has the law of a chordal SLE 4 . Moreover, η is characterized by the property that for all t ≥ 0, the conditional law of h given η | [0 ,t ] is that of a GFF on H \ η ([0 , t ]) with boundary conditions given by - λ on the left side of η ([0 , t ]) and on R - , and by λ on the right side of η ([0 , t ]) and on R + .

The results in [SS13] were generalized in [WW17] as follows. Fix a collection of weights ( ρ L ; ρ R ) satisfying ∑ k i =1 ρ i,L &gt; - 2 for all k ∈ [1 , ℓ ] Z and ∑ k i =1 ρ i,R &gt; - 2 for all k ∈ [1 , r ] Z , and force points ( x L ; x R ), and let h be a GFF on H with boundary conditions given by - λ (1 + ∑ j i =0 ρ i,L ) on [ x j +1 ,L , x j,L ) and λ (1 + ∑ j i =0 ρ i,R ) on [ x j,R , x j +1 ,R ) for j ∈ [0 , ℓ ] Z (resp. j ∈ [0 , r ] Z ), where ρ 0 ,L = ρ 0 ,R = 0, x 0 ,L = 0 - , x ℓ +1 ,L = -∞ , x 0 ,R = 0 + , and x r +1 ,R = ∞ . Then, the zero level line of h from 0 to ∞ can be rigorously defined. Moreover, it has the law of a chordal SLE 4 ( ρ L ; ρ R ) and it is a measurable function of h . Furthermore, the level line is characterized by the property that, for all t ≥ 0, the conditional law of h given η | [0 ,t ] is that of a GFF on H \ η ([0 , t ]) with boundary conditions given by - λ (resp. λ ) on the left (resp. right) side of η ([0 , t ]) and the same boundary values as h on ∂ H .

For all u ∈ R , the level line of h with height u from 0 to ∞ is given by the level line of h - u from 0 to ∞ . Then, we have the following interaction rules for level lines with different heights proved in [WW17].

Theorem 2.1 ([WW17, Theorem 1.1.4 and Remark 1.1.5]) . Suppose that h is a GFF on H with piecewise constant boundary conditions that change only finitely many times. For all u ∈ R and x ∈ ∂ H , we let γ x u be the level line of h with height u starting from x and ending at ∞ (when it exists). Fix x 2 ≤ x 1 .

- (i) If u 2 &lt; u 1 , then γ x 2 u 2 a.s. stays to the left of γ x 1 u 1 (possibly touching it).
- (ii) If u 2 = u 1 , then γ x 2 u 2 may intersect γ x 1 u 1 and, upon intersecting, the two curves merge and never separate.
- (iii) If u 1 - u 2 ≥ 2 λ , then γ x 1 u 1 and γ x 2 u 2 do not intersect each other a.s.
- 2.5. Labeled CLE 4 . Labeled CLE 4 in D , also called the uniform exploration of the CLE 4 in D , was introduced in [WW13] and corresponds to a Markovian exploration of CLE 4 loops, with a label on each loop that keeps track of the time when each loop is discovered. We will briefly describe the construction and refer to [WW13] and [WW17] for more details.

To construct the exploration, we define the measure M as the image measure of µ ⊗ ω under the mapping ( ℓ, x ) ↦→ x · ℓ (the rotation of ℓ by x ), where ω denotes the harmonic measure on ∂ D as seen from 0, and µ denotes the (infinite) SLE 4 bubble measure on D pinned at 1 introduced in [SW12], normalized so that the time at which the loop surrounding 0 is discovered is an exponential random variable of parameter one. We also let ( ℓ t ) t ≥ 0 denote a Poisson point process (PPP) with intensity measure given by M times the Lebesgue measure on R + ( ℓ t being defined only for the countably many atom times t ). This process defines an ordering on the (pinned) loops (also called bubbles), where each loop ℓ is equipped with time label t ℓ .

The exploration targeted at 0 is defined as follows. Let τ 0 denote the first time that the PPP discovers a loop that surrounds 0. For all ε ∈ (0 , 1), we let ℓ t ε 1 , . . . , ℓ t ε nε denote the loops discovered strictly before time τ 0 with diameter at least ε , such that t ε 1 &lt; · · · &lt; t ε n ε . We define D 0 = D and inductively let D i denote the connected component of D i - 1 \ ϕ i ( ℓ t ε i ) containing 0, where ϕ i is the conformal mapping from D onto D i - 1 such that ϕ i (0) = 0 and ϕ ′ i (0) &gt; 0. Then, D n ε converges in the Carathéodory sense as ε → 0 to the interior of a loop which, by [WW13], has the same law as the loop in a CLE 4 surrounding 0. The loop surrounding 0 is then equipped with the time label τ 0 and we say that it is discovered at time τ 0 . More generally, if we set D ε t = D i - 1 when t ε i - 1 ≤ t &lt; t ε i , where t ε 0 def = 0 and t ε n ε +1 def = τ 0 , then for all t &lt; τ 0 , D ε t decreases as ε ↓ 0 and hence converges a.s. in the Carathéodory sense to a simply connected open domain C t (0), which is called the connected component of the unexplored region containing the origin.

It was shown in [WW13, Lemma 6] that the measure M is invariant under Möbius transformations of D . Therefore, by conformal invariance, we can define the exploration targeted at any fixed point z ∈ D by mapping the exploration targeted at 0 via a Möbius transformation of D which maps 0 to z . The transformation is unique up to rotation, but by the rotational invariance of M the law of the exploration does not depend on the choice.

The labeled CLE 4 exploration is then defined by coupling the exploration processes targeted at any two fixed points z, w ∈ D together so that they agree up until the first time that they are separated, and then evolve independently. For all z ∈ D , we denote by C t ( z ) the connected component of the unexplored region containing z . The explored region is the compact set

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where D Q def = D ∩ Q 2 . Since every rational point is encircled by a loop (the loop ℒ ( x ) surrounding x ∈ C t ( z ) being discovered after time t , inside C t ( z )) and since C t ( z ) is open, we can also write C t ( z ) = ⋃ x ∈ C t ( z ) ∩ Q 2 int( ℒ ( x )).

- 2.6. Two-valued sets of the GFF. Next, we introduce the two-valued sets of a GFF h on D that we are going to consider. Recall the definition of a local set of h given in [SS13]. We refer to [ASW19] and [AS18] for more detailed expositions of two-valued sets of the GFF.

Definition 2.2. Fix a, b &gt; 0 and let h be a zero-boundary GFF in a simply connected domain G ⊊ C . We say that A - a,b is a two-valued local set of h of levels - a and b if it is a local set of h and satisfies the following properties.

- (i) If h A - a,b denotes the harmonic part of h on G \ A - a,b (as in Section 2.1), then we have that h A - a,b ∈ {- a, b } a.s.
- (ii) The set A - a,b is a thin local set, i.e., for any smooth test function f ∈ C ∞ 0 ( G ), we have that

a.s.

- (iii) The set A - a,b ∪ ∂G has a finite number of connected components.

It was shown in [ASW19] that if a, b ∈ R are such that - a &lt; 0 &lt; b and a + b ≥ 2 λ , then such a set A - a,b exists and it is a.s. determined by h . Moreover, A - a,b is unique in the sense that if A ′ is another local set of h satisfying (i), (ii) and (iii), we have that A ′ = A - a,b a.s.

Let us now briefly review the construction of A - a, - a +2 λ for a ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), which will be needed for our purposes. We will follow [AS18, Section 3.2].

To begin with, we let η denote the level line of h + a - λ from - i to i (level lines in D being defined by conformal invariance) and set A 1 = η ([0 , ∞ ]). In each connected component O of D \ A 1 lying to the left of η , we let x and y be the two endpoints of the (a.s. nondegenerate) arc ∂O ∩ ∂ D , and we assume that ( - i , x, y ) are in counterclockwise order. We then let η O denote the level line of h | O + a - λ from x to y .

As for the connected components O of D \ A 1 lying to the right of η , we perform an analogous procedure except that we explore the level line of h | O + a - λ from y to x . We denote those level lines by η O .

̸

In the next step of the exploration, we let A 2 denote the closure of the union of A 1 with the η O 's for each connected component O of D \ A 1 . In the connected components of D \ A 2 whose boundaries are contained in A 2 , we stop the exploration. Note that the boundary conditions of h on each of the aforementioned components are a.s. constant and given by either - a or 2 λ - a . In the rest of the components, we iterate as before to obtain A n . We note that if V is a connected component of D \ A 2 such that ∂V ∩ ∂ D = ∅ , then we have that the boundary conditions of h | V are given by 0 on ∂V ∩ ∂ D , and - a (resp. 2 λ - a ) on ∂V \ ∂ D if V is contained in a component of D \ A 1 lying to the right (resp. left) of η . Then, we set A - a, - a +2 λ def = ∪ n ∈ N A n .

- Remark 2.3 . The uniqueness of A - a,b implies that the starting and ending points of the level lines and the order in which they were sampled to produce A - a,b do not matter. Alternatively, this can also be seen from the reversibility of the SLE 4 ( ρ ) processes [WW17, Theorem 1.1.6].
- Remark 2.4 . The set A - a, - a +2 λ constructed above is equal to the range of a clockwise boundary conformal loop ensemble with κ = 4 and parameter ρ (i.e., a BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) process) with ρ = - a/λ introduced in [MSW17, Section 7] (the definition is recalled in Section 5.2). By varying a ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), we obtain BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) for all ρ ∈ ( - 2 , 0). More precisely, the loops with boundary condition 2 λ - a in A - a, - a +2 λ correspond to the true loops of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) and the loops with boundary condition - a in A - a, - a +2 λ correspond to the false loops of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ).

- Fix r ∈ (0 , 2 λ ). For all j ∈ N , we define sets B j r iteratively as follows. First, we set B 1 r def = A - r, - r +2 λ . Then, we let B 2 r denote the closure of the union of the sets explored and note that the boundary conditions of h on the connected components of D \ B 2 r are constant and lie in { 2 λ - r, 2 λ - 2 r, - 2 r } .

Suppose that we have constructed B j r for j ∈ N . Then, in each connected component O of D \ B j r such that the boundary conditions of h | O are given by - jr , we explore the set A - r, - r +2 λ ( O ) defined in the same way as A - r, - r +2 λ but with the zero-boundary GFF h | O - h B j r in place of h . We define B j +1 r as the closure of the union of B j r and the sets explored.

We set B r def = ∪ j ∈ N B j r and B 0 def = ∪ n ∈ N B 2 - n . For every loop ℓ of B 0 (i.e., whose interior int( ℓ ) is a connected component of D \ B 0 ), we define a label t ℓ such that the boundary conditions of h | int( ℓ ) are given by 2 λ - t ℓ (their a.s. constancy on each connected component of D \ B 0 is part of the construction of the two-valued sets in [AS18, Section 6]). Then, we have the following.

Proposition 2.5 ([AS18, Proposition 6.6]) . Suppose that we have the setup described above. Then, the collection of loops in B 0 has the law of a non-nested CLE 4 Γ in D and { ( ℓ, t ℓ ) } ℓ ∈ Γ has the law of a labeled CLE 4 .

Let us provide more details on this coupling. The first lemma shows an inclusion of loops with boundary condition - 2 - k .

Lemma 2.6. Let r ∈ (0 , 2 λ ) . For all m ∈ Z ≥ 0 , the loops with boundary condition - r of B 1 r are loops with boundary condition - r in B 2 m 2 - m r . In particular, for r = 2 - k , n ≥ k and m = n - k , the false loops of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - 2 - k /λ ) (from Remark 2.4) are loops with boundary condition - 2 - k in B 2 n - k 2 - n .

Proof. By [AS18, Subsection 6.2.4], we know that for all r ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), all loops with boundary condition - r of B 1 r are loops with boundary condition - r in B 2 r/ 2 . The desired result follows by induction as detailed below. The case m = 0 is trivial.

Let m ∈ Z ≥ 0 . Let us assume that for all r ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), for all zero-boundary GFFs h on D (equivalently, by conformal invariance, on any simply connected domain U ⊊ C ), all the loops with boundary condition - r in B 1 r (constructed with h ) are loops with boundary condition - r in B 2 m 2 - m r . Let r ∈ (0 , 2 λ ) and fix a zero-boundary GFF h on D . Let O be the interior of a loop with boundary condition - r in B 1 r . By the beginning of the proof, we know that O is also the interior of a loop with boundary condition - r in B 2 r/ 2 . But, by the iterative construction of B 2 r/ 2 , we see that O is included in the interior O ′ of a loop of B 1 r/ 2 with boundary condition - r/ 2 (in the sense that the boundary conditions of h | O ′ are given by - r/ 2) and that O is the interior of a loop of A - r/ 2 , - r/ 2+2 λ ( O ′ ) with boundary condition - r/ 2. Moreover, using that B 1 r/ 2 is a local set for the GFF h , conditionally on B 1 r/ 2 and h B 1 r/ 2 , the field h | O ′ - h B 1 r/ 2 is a zero-boundary GFF on O ′ . Note that A - r/ 2 , - r/ 2+2 λ ( O ′ ) can therefore be seen as B 1 r/ 2 ( O ′ ), in the sense that it is defined in the same way as B 1 r/ 2 except that D , h are replaced by O ′ , h | O ′ - h B 1 r/ 2 . By performing the exact same iterative construction in O ′ (instead of D ) with the field h | O ′ - h B 1 r/ 2 (instead of h ), we define the set B j r/ 2 ( O ′ ) for all j ≥ 1. By induction hypothesis, we deduce that O is the interior of a loop with boundary condition - r/ 2 of B 2 m 2 - m - 1 r ( O ′ ) and that O ′ is the interior of a loop with boundary condition - r/ 2 of B 2 m 2 - m - 1 r . Finally, note that by the iterative construction of B 2 m +1 2 - m - 1 r , we can build B 2 m +1 2 - m - 1 r by first taking B 2 m 2 - m - 1 r and then by exploring B 2 m 2 - m - 1 r ( O ′′ ) in all the interiors O ′′ of the loops with boundary condition - r/ 2 of B 2 m 2 - m - 1 r . In particular, we conclude that O is the interior of a loop with boundary condition - r in B 2 m +1 2 - m - 1 r . This ends the induction step. □

Remark 2.8 . The coupling defined in [AS18], performed with local sets whose loops have boundary conditions determined by dyadic numbers j 2 - k for j, k ∈ Z ≥ 0 , can be performed in the exact same way by multiplying these dyadic numbers by a fixed real number r ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), so that the resulting levels jr 2 - k again range over (0 , 2 λ ). Hence, by taking Proposition 2.5, Lemma 2.6 and Lemma 2.7 (with k = 0) into account, we see that for all t ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), we can couple the uniform exploration of the CLE 4 with a BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - t/λ ) so that the interior of the false BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - t/λ ) loop containing the origin (on the event that the origin is encircled by a false loop) is the connected component of the unexplored region containing 0 at time t . More generally, one can couple the uniform exploration with a BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - t/λ ) so that the interiors of all the false BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - t/λ ) loops are connected components of the unexplored region at time t .

Let us emphasize that, in order to get all the connected components of the unexplored region D \ ℬ t ( ∂ D ), one needs to look at the union described in Lemma 2.7: the interiors of the false BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - t/λ ) loops do not give all the connected components of D \ ℬ t ( ∂ D ). We obtain that the collection of loops in B 2 r/ 2 with boundary condition - r which intersect ∂ D is precisely the family of loops in A - r, 2 λ - r with boundary condition - r .

Next, let us state another consequence of [AS18].

Lemma 2.10. Almost surely, for all t &gt; 0 , the explored region ℬ t ( ∂ D ) is the closure of the union of the domains encircled by loops of the CLE 4 discovered up to time t .

Proof. The statement when t &gt; 0 is dyadic is a consequence of the definition of the sets B t 0 for t ≥ 0 (the superscript in B t 0 , imported from [AS18], is a label cutoff, not a number of iterations as in the notation B j r of Section 2.6) as the closure of the union of the domains encircled by loops of the CLE 4 with boundary condition at least 2 λ - t in [AS18, Definition 6.2] and of the identification of ( B t 0 ) t ≥ 0 with the uniform exploration ( ℬ t ( ∂ D )) t ≥ 0 made in [AS18, Section 6.4.3].

For general t &gt; 0, we view ( ℬ t ( ∂ D )) t ≥ 0 as a càdlàg process taking its values in the space of the compact subsets of D equipped with the Hausdorff distance (left limits exist by monotonicity, and right-continuity holds since the loops discovered in a component of the unexplored region shortly after time t are small and pinned at its boundary). When t &gt; 0 is a time of left continuity of ( ℬ t ( ∂ D )) t ≥ 0 , we obtain directly the result by approximating ℬ t ( ∂ D ) using dyadic times (the dyadic case holding a.s. simultaneously for all dyadic t ). When t is a jump time, then it is a time of a loop discovery in the PPP of loops (the unexplored components change only at such times), so that ℬ t ( ∂ D ) is obtained as the union of the left limit ℬ - t ( ∂ D ) def = ⋃ s&lt;t ℬ s ( ∂ D ) together with the closure of the domain encircled by the loop discovered at time t . This concludes the proof of the lemma. □

2.8. Background on Carathéodory convergence. We gather here some equivalent definitions of Carathéodory convergence. Let ( U n ) n ≥ 0 be a sequence of simply connected domains U n ⊊ C . Let U ⊊ C be a simply connected domain. Let z ∈ U . When z ∈ U n , let f n (resp. f ) be the unique conformal map from D to U n (resp. U ) such that f n (0) = z (resp. f (0) = z ) and f ′ n (0) &gt; 0 (resp. f ′ (0) &gt; 0). We say that ( U n , z ) converges to ( U, z ) as n →∞ in the Carathéodory sense if z ∈ U n for n large enough and f n converges to f uniformly on compact subsets of D . The convergence does not depend on the choice of z ∈ U , so we will often simply say that U n converges to U in the Carathéodory sense.

Let us also recall the original definition of Carathéodory convergence.

Definition 2.11. Let U ⊂ C be a domain, let z ∈ U , and let ( U n ) n ≥ 0 be a sequence of domains in C containing z . The sequence ( U n ) n ≥ 0 converges in the Carathéodory sense toward U with respect to z if:

- For every compact set K ⊂ U , we have K ⊂ U n for all n large enough;
- For every connected open set V ∋ z , if V ⊂ U n for infinitely many n ≥ 0, then V ⊂ U .

The above definition is more general since it does not assume that the domains are simply connected. The equivalence between the two definitions in the case of simply connected domains is the content of the Carathéodory kernel theorem.

Furthermore, using the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, the convergence in the Carathéodory sense implies the uniform convergence of f - 1 n toward f - 1 on every compact subset of U .

We shall repeatedly use the following elementary fact. Let ℒ n → ℒ in the Hausdorff topology, where ℒ is a Jordan curve in D surrounding a fixed point x , as does each ℒ n . Then every compact K ⊆ int( ℒ ) (resp. K ⊆ D \ int( ℒ )) satisfies K ⊆ int( ℒ n ) (resp. K ∩ int( ℒ n ) = ∅ ) for n large. In particular int( ℒ n ) → int( ℒ ). This applies to CLE 4 loops, which are Jordan curves.

We now define the space of domains. We denote by D the space of decreasing families of simply connected domains ( D t ) t ≥ 0 such that for all t ≥ 0, we have 0 ∈ D t and the conformal radius of D t seen from zero is e - t : in other words, if f t is the unique conformal map from D to D t sending 0 to 0 with f ′ t (0) &gt; 0, then f ′ t (0) = e - t .

The space D is equipped with an extension of the Carathéodory topology: if ( D t ) t ≥ 0 , ( ˜ D t ) t ≥ 0 ∈ D and ( f t ) t ≥ 0 , ( ˜ f t ) t ≥ 0 are the associated conformal maps, then we set

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

The metric space ( D , d D ) is complete and separable. See, e.g., [MS16c, Section 6.1].

2.9. Properties of SLEs with force points and BCLEs. Recall that for each t ≥ 0, C t (0) is the connected component of the unexplored region of the uniform exploration containing the origin. This subsection is aimed at proving the following result.

Proposition 2.12. As t → 0 , C t (0) converges in probability in the Carathéodory sense to D (i.e. P [ K ⊆ C t (0)] → 1 for every compact K ⊆ D ) and the maximum of the diameters of the connected components of D \ C t (0) converges in probability to zero. In particular, for all δ &gt; 0 , with probability 1 - o (1) as t → 0 , for all a ∈ ∂ D , there exists x ∈ ∂C t (0) ∩ ∂ D such that | a - x | ≤ δ .

The key result for establishing Proposition 2.12 is the following.

Proposition 2.13. For each a &gt; 0 and ρ ∈ ( - 2 , 0) , let d ρ ( a ) be the supremum of the diameters of the excursions away from R (i.e. the closures of the connected components of γ \ R ) of a chordal SLE 4 ( ρ ; - ρ - 2) γ in H from 0 to ∞ , with force points at 0 - and 0 + , which have an endpoint in [0 , a ] . Then,

Proof. This is a consequence of [DKM24, Proposition 3.1]. We note that this result is stated and proved for κ ∈ (0 , 4) using the tools from [MS16a]. The same argument, however, works for κ = 4 using the results from [WW17]. The statement of [DKM24, Proposition 3.1] does not explicitly mention that the diameter of the components tends to 0 as ρ ↑ 0; however, this is implicit in the proof of [DKM24, Lemma 3.8]. □

Proposition 2.12 follows from the definition of BCLEs using SLEs with force points and from the coupling of Section 2.7.

Proof of Proposition 2.12 assuming Proposition 2.13. Let h denote the zero-boundary GFF used to generate the uniform CLE 4 exploration as in Section 2.7. Let γ be a chordal SLE 4 ( ρ ; - 2 - ρ ) curve in D from - 1 to 1 which is the branch of the exploration tree of a BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) process Γ in D . Let γ ′ be the chordal SLE 4 ( ρ ; - 2 - ρ ) from 1 to - 1 in D of the BCLE ⟲ 4 ( - 2 - ρ ) process Γ ′ in D where Γ, Γ ′ are coupled together so that the true (resp. false) loops of Γ coincide with the false (resp. true) loops of Γ ′ .

Combining Proposition 2.13 with the reversibility of SLE 4 ( ρ ; - ρ - 2) processes [WW17, Theorem 1.1.6] (which transfers to the arcs near the target point the control near the starting point), the maximum of the diameters of the arcs of γ touching { e i θ : θ ∈ [ π, 2 π ] } and of the arcs of γ ′ touching { e i θ : θ ∈ [0 , π ] } converges to zero in probability as ρ ↑ 0. But [AS18, Lemma 3.6] combined with the level line interaction rules [WW17, Theorem 1.1.4] implies that every arc of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) loop around the origin is then one of these arcs so that the maximum of the diameters of the arcs of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) false loop around the origin tends to zero in probability as ρ ↑ 0.

Moreover, another consequence of [AS18] is that with probability going to 1 as ρ ↑ 0, the loop of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) around the origin is a false loop (see, e.g., [LSYZ24, Theorem 1.10] for more details).

Next, by Remark 2.8, for all t ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), C t (0) is the interior of the false BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - t/λ ) loop around the origin on an event of probability 1 - o (1) as t → 0. Hence, for all δ &gt; 0, with probability 1 - o (1), all the arcs of ∂C t (0) away from ∂ D have diameter at most δ . On this event, ∂C t (0) lies within distance δ of ∂ D , so B 1 - δ (0) ⊆ C t (0); every a ∈ ∂ D is within distance 2 δ of ∂C t (0) ∩ ∂ D (consider the point of ∂C t (0) on [0 , a ] and an endpoint of the arc containing it); and every component of D \ C t (0), bounded by one such arc and the arc of ∂ D between its endpoints, has diameter at most 3 δ . This proves the three assertions. □

## 3. When the boundary is identified with a point

In this section, we construct the metric D ∂ as a subsequential scaling limit of the graph distance on the non-simple CLE when the boundary ∂ D is also seen as a point of the metric space. In particular, we prove that the scaling limits of the balls from the boundary are given by the balls of the uniform exploration.

For all deterministic x ∈ C , we shall write ℒ κ ( x ) for the loop of ̂ Γ κ that surrounds x (with the convention that ℒ κ ( x ) = ∂ D for x / ∈ D ), which exists a.s. The second equality is a consequence of the main result of [ASYZ25]; the particular formula for a κ is not important for this paper, however, as we just need that a κ →∞ as κ ↓ 4, which also follows directly from the fact that CLE 4 loops a.s. do not touch the domain boundary. The assertion made just before (3.1) follows from the domain Markov property of the CLE κ . Indeed, it follows from [She09, Section 5.1] that if we condition on the closure χ of the union of the CLE κ loops which intersect ∂ D , then the law of the remaining loops is given by an independent CLE κ in each connected component of D \ χ . So, if ℒ κ (0) ∩ ∂ D = ∅ , then we can iterate inside the connected component U 1 of D \ χ containing 0. By conformal invariance, we still have that the conditional probability that ℒ κ (0) ∩ ∂U 1 = ∅ is equal to 1 / a κ . If ℒ κ (0) ∩ ∂U 1 = ∅ , then we can iterate the above procedure and at each step, we have that the conditional probability that ℒ κ (0) intersects the domain boundary is equal to 1 / a κ . It follows that D κ,∂ ( ℒ κ (0) , ∂ D ) has the law of a geometric random variable with success probability given by (3.1). By conformal invariance, the same is true for the loop surrounding a point x ∈ D .

For all t ≥ 0 and ℒ ∈ ̂ Γ κ , let us denote by ℬ κ,∂ t ( ℒ ) the closure of the union of the domains encircled by the loops ℒ ′ ∈ ̂ Γ κ which are at D κ,∂ -distance at most t from ℒ . Similarly, for all z ∈ D , let us denote by ℒ κ ( z ) the loop of the CLE κ which is drawn by the curve η κ z around z when it exists. We note that for arbitrary z ∈ D , the loop ℒ κ ( z ) exists if and only if the curve η κ z makes a counterclockwise loop around z .

̸

The CLE κ can then be constructed as the countable collection of (non-simple) loops ℒ κ ( z ) for z ∈ D Q ; that this collection satisfies the axioms of Section 2.3 is the content of [SW12, She09]. It is made of non-simple loops which can touch each other as well as the boundary of D , and we equip it with the graph distance of Section 3.1. By [She09, Lemma 5.2], we know that the loop ℒ κ ( z ) touches ∂ D if and only if η κ z has not drawn a clockwise loop around z before drawing the counterclockwise loop around z . Moreover, when η κ z draws its first clockwise loop around z , the encircled region is either a connected component of the complement of a loop of the CLE κ or a connected component of the complement of a chain of loops of the CLE κ , i.e. of a finite sequence of loops each of which intersects the next. Since this loop or chain was drawn before that first time, it touches ∂ D . The same applies to each subsequent clockwise loop, with ∂ D replaced by the boundary of the region encircled by the previous one. See Figure 2 for an illustration.

Using the fact that the times τ κ 1 , τ κ 2 , . . . at which η κ z draws a clockwise loop around z are renewal times, in the sense that if we conformally map the encircled region to D sending z to 0 and the tip of η κ z to 1, then the image of the remainder of the curve is independent of what has been drawn before and has the same law as η κ 0 , we can see by induction that for all k ≥ 1, the loop ℒ κ ( z ) is at graph distance k from ∂ D if and only if the counterclockwise loop around z is drawn by η κ z between times τ κ k - 1 and τ κ k , where by convention we set τ κ 0 = 0.

Remark 3.9 . Note that by Corollary 3.8, the time of discovery of a loop ℒ ( y ) by the uniform exploration from ℒ ( x ) is the limit of a - 1 κ n D κ n ,∂ ( ℒ κ n ( x ) , ℒ κ n ( y )) as n → ∞ , so it corresponds to D ∂ ( ℒ ( x ) , ℒ ( y )). ## 4. When the boundary is not a point

The purpose of this section is to construct another metric D , coupled with the objects D ∂ , ℬ ∂ , K ∂ constructed in Section 3, in which the boundary is not identified with a point of the metric space. We follow the same recipe as in Section 3 except that the metric balls are not directly described by the uniform exploration. The corresponding distances, metric balls, and geodesics will be denoted as before, but without the ∂ superscript.

4.1. Tightness of the distances, geodesics and metric balls at rational times. Let D κ be the graph distance on Γ κ , with the same adjacency relation as in Section 3.1 but without the extra vertex ∂ D . Note that Γ κ need not be connected as a graph, so D κ could a priori be infinite; it is a.s. finite by the stochastic domination established in the proof of Proposition 4.1.

The remainder of the present section is devoted to the study of metric balls: we prove that loops are dense in balls and we extend the definition of metric balls to irrational times.

## 4.3. Definition of metric balls for all times: Skorokhod convergence.

Lemma 4.7. For all t ∈ Q ≥ 0 and for all α ∈ 𝒮 ′ , a.s., for all ℒ ∈ Γ , the compact set ℬ t ( α ) (resp. ℬ t ( ℒ ) ) is the closure of the union of the regions encircled by the loops of Γ at D -distance at most t from α (resp. ℒ ).

Proof. First, note that by (4.1), every loop which is included in ℬ t ( α ) is at D -distance at most t from α . Moreover, again by (4.1), every loop which is at distance strictly less than t from α is included in ℬ t ( α ). But by right-continuity (consequence of Lemma 4.6), we deduce that all the loops which are at distance at most t from α are included in ℬ t ( α ). As a result, the closure of the union of the domains encircled by the loops at D -distance at most t from α is included in ℬ t ( α ).

It remains to prove the other inclusion. One can first see that for all x in the interior of ℬ t ( α ), there exists a sequence ( x k ) k ≥ 1 in Q 2 in the interior of ℬ t ( α ) such that x k → x as k →∞ . Then, ℒ ( x k ) ⊆ ℬ t ( α ) for all k (because otherwise we cannot have x k ∈ ℬ t ( α ) by (4.1)). Moreover, x is arbitrarily close to the union of the regions encircled by the ℒ ( x k )'s. Thus, the interior of ℬ t ( α ) is included in the closure of the union of the domains encircled by the loops at distance at most t from α .

To conclude, it remains to check that any point on the boundary can be approached by loops at distance at most t from α . This is a direct consequence of the two points of Lemma 4.5 and of the analogous property for the uniform exploration (see Remark 3.9). The exact same argument works to prove the claim of the lemma with a loop ℒ in place of α , since Remark 3.9 and Lemmas 4.5 and 4.6 still hold with ℒ in place of α . □

## 5. Large non-simple CLE loops are fat

This section can be skipped on a first reading. Its goal is to establish a strengthened convergence for the loops of a non-simple CLE as κ ′ ↓ 4 (Lemma 5.1), which will be a crucial tool in the subsequent sections for verifying that our subsequential limiting metric satisfies the axioms of Definition 1.2. Throughout this section, we will denote the parameter of the non-simple CLE by κ ′ ∈ (4 , 8) and we will write κ = 16 /κ ′ for the parameter of the corresponding simple CLE. The sequence which was denoted by κ n in the previous sections will be denoted by κ ′ n here. We already know from (4.1) that the loop surrounding any given rational point converges in the Hausdorff topology as κ ′ ↓ 4. We will strengthen this convergence by showing that the closure of the union of loops intersecting a given compact subset of D also converges in the Hausdorff topology as κ ′ ↓ 4. Proving this strengthening is highly non-trivial because it requires us to rule out the existence of macroscopic but 'skinny' loops uniformly in κ ′ as κ ′ ↓ 4. The bulk of this section is therefore devoted to proving Proposition 5.2, a result of independent interest which states that large non-simple CLE loops are 'fat', meaning they must contain a large Euclidean ball.

Lemma 5.1. Let K ⊂ D be a connected compact subset of D . For each n ≥ 1 , let F n (resp. F ) be the closure of the union of K and the domains encircled by the loops of Γ κ ′ n (resp. Γ 4 ) which intersect K . Then, jointly with (4.1) and (4.7) , we have

Such a result can be seen as a consequence of [MW17, Lemmas 4.4 and 4.5]: let f t = g t - W t be the associated centered Loewner flow and consider the stopping times τ k = inf { t ≥ 0 : dist( η ( t ) , K ) ≤ 2 - k } for all k ≥ 1. By choosing δ small, a.s. for infinitely many k , we have that f τ k ( K ) ∩ D is included in { z ∈ H : arg( z ) ∈ ( δ, π - δ ) } , so that the curve η will have points of K on its left and right sides.

But, note that by Proposition 5.2, with probability at least 1 - ε , we have F ε n = F ε,δ n . The same is true for F ε and F ε,δ by our choice of δ . By taking (5.1) into account, this concludes the proof. □

The same proof shows that the lemma remains true if the loop surrounding a fixed z 0 ∈ D Q is excluded from F n and F : this only removes from ( δ/ 2) Z 2 ∩ D the points surrounded by ℒ κ ′ n ( z 0 ), and for each fixed such z , by Section 2.8, for n large ℒ κ ′ n ( z 0 ) surrounds z if and only if ℒ 4 ( z 0 ) does. We use this with z 0 = 0 in Section 6.

The proof of Proposition 5.2 will be achieved by combining the following three steps.

- (i) We will prove the same result as in the statement of Proposition 5.2 but with CLE κ ′ n replaced by CLE κ n in D with κ n = 16 /κ ′ n . This result is much easier because CLE κ for κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4] can be coupled on a common probability space so as to be monotone in κ [SW12].
- (ii) We will use a specific coupling of CLE κ ′ n , CLE κ n , and a GFF on D which comes from the results of [MSW17], which ensures that a.s. whenever a CLE κ ′ n loop intersects a CLE κ n loop, it has to surround it.
- (iii) Under the coupling from (ii), we have that the following holds with probability tending to 1 as n →∞ . Every CLE κ ′ n loop with large Euclidean diameter has to intersect a CLE κ n loop with large Euclidean diameter. We will prove this by showing that large CLE κ ′ n loops cannot stay close to ∂ D and therefore must cross a macroscopic annulus strictly contained in D . We will then explore the simple CLE κ n loops crossing this annulus to form a mesh of conformal rectangles, and show that it is highly unlikely for a non-simple loop to cross such a rectangle without intersecting its simple CLE boundaries.

The claim in item (i) will be shown in Section 5.1. In Section 5.2, we will describe the coupling between the CLE κ ′ n , the CLE κ n , and the GFF on D that we are going to use. Finally in Section 5.3, we will prove the claim in item (iii) and hence complete the proof of Proposition 5.2.

5.1. Big simple CLE loops are fat. In this section, we will prove that with probability tending to 1 as κ ↑ 4, we have that every loop in a non-nested CLE κ in D with large Euclidean diameter surrounds a Euclidean ball with large Euclidean diameter.

For all κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4], let Γ κ be a non-nested CLE κ in D . In this subsection, we have two main results. The first is the following.

Proposition 5.3. Let ( κ n ) n ≥ 1 be a sequence in (8 / 3 , 4) with κ n ↑ 4 . For all ε &gt; 0 such that a.s. no loop of Γ 4 has diameter exactly ε (all but countably many ε , since ε ↦→ E [# { ℒ ∈ Γ 4 : diam( ℒ ) ≥ ε } ] is finite and non-increasing, hence has countably many discontinuities), the number of loops of Γ κ n with diameter at least ε converges in law as n → ∞ to the number of loops of Γ 4 with diameter at least ε . Moreover, for all ε, ε ′ &gt; 0 , there exists δ &gt; 0 (depending on ε , ε ′ , and on the sequence ( κ n ) n ≥ 1 ) such that for all n ≥ 1 , with probability at least 1 - ε ′ , all the loops of Γ κ n with diameter at least ε encircle a Euclidean ball of radius δ .

Let us now turn to state our second main result. Let us first introduce some notation. Fix 0 &lt; s &lt; t and z ∈ D such that the annulus A = A s,t ( z ) = B t ( z ) \ B s ( z ) ⊆ D . Given ℒ ∈ Γ κ , the segments of ℒ in A are the closures of the connected components of ℒ ∩ A ; each has both endpoints on ∂A . Such a segment is a crossing if its endpoints lie on different components of ∂A , and an excursion from the outer (resp. inner) boundary if they both lie on ∂B t ( z ) (resp. ∂B s ( z )); ℒ crosses A if one of its segments is a crossing. A conformal rectangle is a simply connected domain with four marked prime ends, splitting its boundary into its top, bottom, left and right boundaries. Let Γ κ, out A (resp. Γ κ, in A ) denote the collection of segments of loops in Γ κ which make an excursion from the outer (resp. inner) boundary of A . Let also Γ κ A denote the collection of loops in Γ κ which cross A .

Next, for all ℒ ∈ Γ κ, out A ∪ Γ κ, in A , we denote by int( ℒ ) the intersection of the region encircled by the associated loop and A . In particular, distinct segments of one and the same loop have the same int. We will prove the following result that we are going to need in Section 5.3.

## Proposition 5.4. We have the following convergences:

- (i) The number N κ of crossings of A by loops in Γ κ A converges in law to the number N 4 of crossings of A by loops in Γ 4 A as κ ↑ 4 . More precisely, for all κ n ↑ 4 there exists an ordering of the crossings η n 1 , . . . , η n N κn of Γ κ n A for which the random vectors ( N κ n , η n 1 , . . . , η n N κn ) converge in law to ( N 4 , η 1 , . . . , η N 4 ) , where η 1 , . . . , η N 4 are the crossings of A by loops in Γ 4 A , for the topology of Hausdorff convergence.
- (ii) Jointly with the above convergence, the compact sets ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ κn, out A int( ℒ ) and ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ κn, in A int( ℒ ) converge in distribution for the Hausdorff distance to ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ 4 , out A int( ℒ ) and ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ 4 , in A int( ℒ ) .
- (iii) In particular, the conformal rectangles whose left and right sides are given by the crossings of A by loops in Γ κ n A and whose top and bottom sides are respectively included in ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ κn, out A ℒ and ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ κn, in A ℒ converge in distribution as n →∞ in the Carathéodory sense (jointly with their corners) to the conformal rectangles whose left and right sides are given by the crossings of A by loops in Γ 4 A and whose top and bottom sides are respectively included in ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ 4 , out A ℒ and ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ 4 , in A ℒ , and their moduli are tight in (0 , ∞ ) .

To prove the above results, let us use an appropriate coupling. Let ( S κ ) κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4] be an increasing coupling of Brownian loop soups on D such that S κ has intensity c κ = (3 κ - 8)(6 - κ ) / (2 κ ). Recall from [SW12, Theorem 1.6] that the outer boundaries of the clusters of the loops of S κ form a non-nested CLE κ denoted by Γ κ . Note that in this coupling, for all 8 / 3 &lt; κ 1 ≤ κ 2 ≤ 4, every loop of Γ κ 1 is surrounded by a loop of Γ κ 2 .

In order to give the proof of Proposition 5.4, we will first need to prove a number of preparatory lemmas.

- (i) In Section 5.1.1, we will prove a result related to the convergence of the origin-surrounding loop, as a path, as κ m ↑ 4.
- (ii) In Section 5.1.2, we will prove a result related to the convergence of the large loops as κ m ↑ 4. This will, in particular, give us Proposition 5.3.
- (iii) In Section 5.1.3, we will prove the convergence as κ m ↑ 4 of the crossings of an annulus. This will, in particular, give us Proposition 5.4.

Hence, with probability at least 1 - 2 ε ′ - o (1), the loops of Γ κ n crossing A are ℒ κ n ( X 1 ) , . . . , ℒ κ n ( X N ), where ℒ 4 ( X 1 ) , . . . , ℒ 4 ( X N ) are those of Γ 4 crossing A . Finally, the number of crossings of A by ℒ κ n ( X i ) converges in law to that by ℒ 4 ( X i ): along any subsequence, Lemma 5.6 gives a further subsequence and a coupling in which ℒ κ m ( X i ) is, up to a piece of diameter at most ε , a uniformly converging curve, and ℒ 4 ( X i ) is a.s. not tangent to ∂A . The second part stems from the Hausdorff convergence of the loops. □

Proof of Proposition 5.4. We work using the same coupling as in the proof of Proposition 5.11. The first item is already shown in Proposition 5.11. Let us prove the convergence of ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ κn, out A int( ℒ ); the second one is proved using the same reasoning. Let ε &gt; 0. Let K n ε (resp. K ε ) be the closure of the union of the outer boundary of the annulus A together with the int( ℒ )'s for ℒ ∈ Γ κ n , out A (resp. ℒ ∈ Γ 4 , out A ) which have a diameter at least ε . Note that the Hausdorff distance between K n ε (resp. K ε ) and ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ κn, out A int( ℒ ) (resp. ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ 4 , out A int( ℒ )) is at most ε . Therefore, it suffices to show that K n ε → K ε in probability.

By Proposition 5.3, we know that for all ε ′ &gt; 0, with probability at least 1 - ε ′ , all the loops with diameter at least ε encircle a Euclidean ball of radius δ . By considering a grid ( δ/ 2) Z 2 ∩ D and using Lemma 5.5 (together with the same coupling as in Proposition 5.11), we obtain the Hausdorff convergence of all the loops with diameter at least ε in probability. We also get the convergence K n ε → K ε in probability and this ends the proof of the second item of the proposition. Finally, (iii) follows from (i) and (ii): a compact subset of a limiting rectangle is at positive distance from the limiting bounding sets, hence lies in a prelimit rectangle for n large, and a connected open set lying in prelimit rectangles for infinitely many n meets none of the limiting bounding sets; the corners converge with the crossings. The moduli are tight since a.s. each limiting rectangle contains a channel of fixed width joining its top and bottom sides and one joining its left and right sides;

slightly shortened, these lie in the prelimit rectangles for n large and still reach their sides, and their extremal lengths bound the moduli. □

- 5.2. Coupling of the GFF with simple and non-simple CLEs. Before proceeding to the proof of Proposition 5.2, let us review the coupling between the simple and non-simple CLEs and the GFF that we will consider. We start by recalling the coupling between BCLEs and GFF which is detailed in [MSW17, Section 8] and which is summarized in [MSW17, Tables 1 and 2].

Let κ ∈ (2 , 4] and ρ ∈ ( - 2 , κ - 4], where ρ = κ - 4 is understood in the degenerate sense that the BCLE ⟳ κ ( κ - 4) consists of the single true loop given by the domain boundary (cf. Remark 5.17). Let Γ be a branching SLE κ ( ρ ; κ - 6 - ρ ) process starting from 0 and targeting all other boundary points. If we equip Γ with the orientation from 0 toward other boundary points, then Γ is referred to as a BCLE ⟳ κ ( ρ ). Moreover, the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) loops formed from Γ are referred to as the true loops (resp. false loops) of the BCLE ⟳ κ ( ρ ). We shall refer to a BCLE ⟳ κ ( κ - 6 - ρ ) as a BCLE ⟲ κ ( ρ ); to the true loops (resp. false loops) of BCLE ⟳ κ ( κ - 6 - ρ ) as the false loops (resp. true loops) of BCLE ⟲ κ ( ρ ).

Moreover, for any true (resp. false) loop ℒ , if we write x for the first (resp. last) point on ℒ and y for the last (resp. first) point on ℒ visited by the loop from 0 to 0 that traces the domain boundary ∂ H = R ∪{∞} counterclockwise and φ for a conformal mapping from H onto the interior of ℒ with φ (0) = x and φ ( ∞ ) = y , then Ψ ◦ φ - χ arg( φ ′ ) has the law of a GFF on H with boundary values

Moreover, the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) loops formed from Γ ′ are referred to as the true loops (resp. false loops) of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ ( ρ ′ ).
