Then it follows immediately from Remark 5.15 that we may couple the simple and non-simple BCLEs of Theorem 5.14 with Ψ so that they arise as branching flow lines and branching counterflow lines of Ψ, respectively.

- 5.3. First part of the proof of Proposition 5.2: strategy and construction of the exploration. In this subsection, we are going to construct an exploration in order to prove Proposition 5.2. Let us first give a brief overview of the main strategy of the proof.

By covering D using Euclidean annuli centered at a sufficiently dense set of points, it suffices to prove that the following is true (this covering argument is carried out in the proof of Lemma 5.37). Fix 0 &lt; s &lt; t and z ∈ D , and consider the annulus A s,t ( z ) = B t ( z ) \ B s ( z ) ⊆ D . First, we note that for every ε &gt; 0, by taking t sufficiently close to s , we obtain that with probability at least 1 - ε for all n large enough (on the event that there is no loop in the CLE κ n surrounding ∂B s ( z )), there exists a loop in the CLE κ n crossing A s,t ( z ). Therefore, it suffices to show that with probability tending to 1 as n → ∞ , we have that whenever a CLE κ ′ n loop crosses A s,t ( z ), it has to intersect one of the CLE κ n loops crossing A s,t ( z ).

Let V n 1 , . . . , V n N n denote the conformal rectangles obtained by removing from A s,t ( z ) the loops in the CLE κ n intersecting ∂B s ( z ) ∪ ∂B t ( z ). Note that we have already shown in Proposition 5.4 that V n j converges to V j as n →∞ in the Carathéodory sense, where V 1 , . . . , V N denote the conformal rectangles obtained by removing from A s,t ( z ) the loops in the CLE 4 intersecting ∂B s ( z ) ∪ ∂B t ( z ).

We will show in Section 5.3.1 (see Lemma 5.19) that the following is true. With probability tending to 1 as n →∞ , we have that a loop of a CLE κ ′ n in V n j intersecting the top and bottom boundaries of V n j has to intersect either the left or the right boundary of V n j . In particular, it intersects some loop in the original CLE κ n in D . We would like to use this result to prove Proposition 5.2. However, the restriction of the original CLE κ ′ n in D to V n j does not have the law of a CLE κ ′ n in V n j . To get around that issue, we will introduce in Sections 5.3.2 and 5.3.3 a Markovian way to explore the loops of the CLE κ n in D that intersect ∂B s ( z ) ∪ ∂B t ( z ), which has the following properties.

- (i) At each step, all the simple loops contained in the unexplored region are part of the original CLE κ n in D .
- (ii) Every segment of a loop in the original CLE κ ′ n in D which is contained in the unexplored region is a segment of a loop in a CLE κ ′ n in the unexplored region (see Lemma 5.25).

The above properties of the exploration imply that any segment of a loop in the original CLE κ ′ n in D that makes a crossing between the top and bottom boundaries of the conformal rectangle V n j is part of a loop of a CLE κ ′ n in V n j . Therefore, we will complete the proof of Proposition 5.2 in Section 5.4 by combining with Lemma 5.19, the Carathéodory convergence of V n j to V j as n →∞ (Proposition 5.4), and the fact that in our coupling between CLE κ n and CLE κ ′ n , we have that the following holds a.s. (see Lemma 5.30). Every loop in the CLE κ ′ n intersecting some loop in the CLE κ n has to surround it.

- 5.3.1. Unlikeliness of non-simple CLE loops crossing a rectangle. Next, we show that as κ ′ ↓ 4, it is very unlikely that there exists a loop ℒ in a CLE κ ′ in a rectangle (0 , L ) × (0 , 1) for a fixed L &gt; 0, such that ℒ makes a crossing of the rectangle between its top and bottom boundaries without intersecting either its left or right boundaries. In particular, we will prove the following.

Lemma 5.19. Let L n → L &gt; 0 and κ ′ n ↓ 4 . For each n , write R n def = { z ∈ C : 0 &lt; ℜ ( z ) &lt; L n and 0 &lt; ℑ ( z ) &lt; 1 } and let Γ ′ n be a CLE κ ′ n in R n . Then the probability that there exists a segment of a loop of Γ ′ n that touches the top and bottom sides of R n but neither the left nor the right side of R n goes to 0 as n →∞ .

Equivalently, by conformal invariance, it suffices to show that the following is true: Let I n , J n be connected arcs of ∂ D such that I n → I and J n → J in the Hausdorff sense with dist( I, J ) &gt; 0. Let Γ ′ n be a CLE κ ′ n in D . Then the probability that there exists a segment of a loop of Γ ′ n that touches both I n and J n but not ∂ D \ ( I n ∪ J n ) goes to 0 as n →∞ .

One of the main ingredients in the proof of Lemma 5.19 is the following lemma. It states that with probability tending to 1 as n →∞ , we have that every loop in Γ ′ n intersecting ∂ D has to stay close to ∂ D in the Hausdorff sense.

Lemma 5.20. For each n , write B n for the closure of the union of the loops of Γ ′ n that touch ∂ D . Then B n converges in probability to ∂ D with respect to the Hausdorff topology. That is to say, for each ε &gt; 0 , it holds with probability tending to one as n →∞ that B n ⊂ B ε ( ∂ D ) .

Proof. We will use the same notation as in Proposition 4.1. First, we note that Proposition 2.12 implies that ℬ t ( ∂ D ) converges in probability to ∂ D as t → 0 with respect to the Hausdorff metric induced by the Euclidean metric. Moreover, Proposition 4.1 (applied along a subsequence of any given subsequence of ( κ ′ n ), which suffices) implies that ℬ κ n a κn t ( ∂ D ) converges in distribution to ℬ t ( ∂ D ) as n →∞ with respect to the Hausdorff metric; since { K : d H ( K,∂ D ) &lt; ε } is open, the Portmanteau theorem gives lim inf n P [ ℬ κ n a κn t ( ∂ D ) ⊆ B ε ( ∂ D )] ≥ P [ d H ( ℬ t ( ∂ D ) , ∂ D ) &lt; ε ]. Therefore, we can conclude the proof of the lemma by combining with the fact that B n ⊆ ℬ κ n a κn t ( ∂ D ) as soon as a κ n t ≥ 1, which holds for n large enough for any fixed t &gt; 0. □

Thus, it remains to rule out the bad situations illustrated in Figure 4. To do this, we will construct 'shields' of flow lines of the GFF used to generate Γ ′ n which prevent the pathological cases illustrated in Figure 4 from occurring. Note that the local behavior of flow lines is a.s. determined by the GFF generating them. Hence, to prove that we have sufficiently many 'shields' of flow lines with high probability, we will use the following lemma.

Lemma 5.21. For each M &gt; 0 , p, q ∈ (0 , 1) , and s &gt; 0 , there exists n ∗ = n ∗ ( M,p,q,s ) ∈ N such that the following is true. Let x 1 , . . . , x n ∈ ∂ D with n ≥ n ∗ . Let r &gt; 0 . Suppose that | x j - x k | ≥ 2( s + 1) r for all distinct j, k ∈ [1 , n ] Z . Let also Ψ be a GFF on D with boundary conditions given by a measurable function bounded by M . Let E 1 , . . . , E n be a family of events such that each E j is measurable with respect to the σ -algebra generated by Ψ | B r ( x j ) ∩ D . Suppose that P [ E j ] ≥ p for all j ∈ [1 , n ] Z . Then

Proof. This follows from the spatial Markov property of the GFF, using an argument similar to the one applied in the proof of [GM21b, Lemma 2.7]. Let ˜ U j def = B (1+ s ) r ( x j ) ∩ D and U j def = B r ( x j ) ∩ D . By the Markov property, Ψ | ˜ U j = h j + φ j with φ j harmonic on ˜ U j and the h j independent zeroboundary GFFs on ˜ U j , independent of ( φ j ) j . By scaling and the buffer of width sr between U j and ∂ ˜ U j \ ∂ D , the random variable S j def = sup B (1+ s/ 2) r ( x j ) ∩ D | φ j - E φ j | has tails bounded uniformly in j and r , so we may fix C = C ( q, s ) with P [ G j ] ≥ 1 - (1 - q ) / 4, where G j def = { S j ≤ C } . If φ, φ ′ both satisfy this bound, the laws of ( h j + φ ) | U j and ( h j + φ ′ ) | U j are mutually absolutely continuous with a Radon-Nikodym derivative of second moment bounded in terms of C and s (Cameron-Martin, applied to ( φ - φ ′ ) χ with χ equal to 1 on U j and supported in B (1+ s/ 2) r ( x j )). Since P [ E j ] ≥ p , Cauchy-Schwarz gives P [ E j | φ j ] ≥ c ( p, q, s ) &gt; 0 on G j . The E j being conditionally independent given ( φ j ) j , and at least n/ 2 of the G j occurring with probability at least 1 - (1 - q ) / 2, we get P [ ⋃ j E j ] ≥ 1 - (1 - q ) / 2 - (1 - c ) n/ 2 ≥ q for n large. □

We have shown in Lemma 5.20 that non-simple loops of Γ ′ n that intersect ∂ D have to stay close to ∂ D when n → ∞ . Therefore, the two bad scenarios that have to be dealt with here (as in the proof of [GM21b, Lemma 2.7]) are the events that a segment of a non-simple loop joins I n to J n through the left or right-hand side of the green annulus A 1 - ε n , 1 (0).

Let us now mention a useful consequence of [Leh23, Proposition 2.6].

Lemma 5.22. Let κ n → κ &gt; 0 , ρ n 1 → ρ 1 &gt; - 2 and ρ n 2 → ρ 2 &gt; - 2 with κ n &gt; 0 , ρ n 1 &gt; - 2 and ρ n 2 &gt; - 2 for all n ≥ 1 . Let f n t : H \ K n t → H (resp. f t : H \ K t → H ) for t ≥ 0 be the Loewner chain associated with a chordal SLE κ n ( ρ n 1 ; ρ n 2 ) (resp. SLE κ ( ρ 1 ; ρ 2 ) ) from 0 to ∞ with the force points located at 0 - and 0 + respectively, where K n t (resp. K t ) is the associated compact hull. Then, there is a coupling of the ( f n t ) t ≥ 0 's with ( f t ) t ≥ 0 such that a.s.,

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

uniformly on [0 , T ] × ( H \ B ε ( K T )) for all T, ε &gt; 0 .

Proof. The result from [Leh23, Proposition 2.6] establishes that as κ n → κ and ρ n i → ρ i , the driving functions W n of the SLE κ n ( ρ n 1 ; ρ n 2 ) processes converge in distribution in the local uniform topology to the driving function W of the SLE κ ( ρ 1 ; ρ 2 ) process. By Skorokhod's representation theorem, we can construct a coupling of these processes such that this local uniform convergence of the driving functions holds a.s. Under this coupling, we have almost sure uniform convergence of W n → W on [0 , T ] for any T &gt; 0. According to [Law05, Proposition 4.47], the uniform convergence of driving functions on compact time intervals implies that the corresponding Loewner maps f n t converge to f t uniformly on [0 , T ] × ( H \ B ε ( K T )) for any T, ε &gt; 0. This completes the proof. □

In the following, write κ n def = 16 /κ ′ n . Thus, κ n ↑ 4. Let us now define the main event involving flow lines that we are going to consider and prove that it occurs with positive probability.

Lemma 5.23. Fix distinct x, y ∈ ∂ D and let ε ∈ (0 , 1) be such that ε &lt; min { 1 / 2 , | x - y | / 3 } . For each n ∈ N , let η n be a chordal SLE κ n ( - 1; - 1) in D starting from x and targeting y , where the force points are located at x - and x + respectively. Then, there exists a universal constant p ∈ (0 , 1) such that the following holds for all n ∈ N . With probability at least p , we have that η n hits B ε ((1 - 2 ε ) x ) before exiting B 2 ε ( x ) .

Proof. Let η denote a chordal SLE 4 ( - 1; - 1) process in D from x to y with the force points located at x - and x + respectively, and let ε ∈ (0 , 1) be as in the lemma statement. Note that combining [MW17, Lemma 2.8] with the conformal invariance of the laws of SLE κ ( ρ L ; ρ R ) processes and the Koebe distortion theorem (the conformal map to H sending x to 0 and y to ∞ distorts the balls B 2 ε ( x ), B ε ((1 - 2 ε ) x ) by a bounded factor, uniformly in ε ), we obtain that it suffices to prove the claim in the lemma statement in the case that ε = 1 / 3. Henceforth, assume that ε = 1 / 3.

̸

For each n , we parameterize the curve η n according to half-plane capacity as seen from y , and let ( K n t ) t ≥ 0 denote the family of the corresponding hulls in D . We parameterize η and define the family of hulls ( K t ) t ≥ 0 for η similarly. Let f t : D \ K t → D (resp. f n t : D \ K n t → D ) be the Loewner chain associated with ( K t ) t ≥ 0 (resp. ( K n t ) t ≥ 0 ). Let σ be the hitting time of B 1 / 6 ( x/ 3) by η and E the event that σ precedes the exit time of B 2 / 3 ( x ); by [MW17, Lemmas 2.3 and 2.8] and the conformal invariance of the law of η , P [ E ] ≥ p for some universal p ∈ (0 , 1). Couple ( η n ) n ∈ N and η as in Lemma 5.22. On E , K σ is a compact subset of B 2 / 3 ( x ) meeting B 1 / 6 ( x/ 3), so the convergence f n → f , which gives K n σ ⊆ B δ ( K σ ) for every δ &gt; 0, yields for n large that K n σ ⊆ B 2 / 3 ( x ) and K n σ ∩ B 1 / 3 ( x/ 3) = ∅ , i.e. η n hits B 1 / 3 ( x/ 3) before exiting B 2 / 3 ( x ). For the finitely many remaining n , the probability is positive by the same lemmas. □

We are now ready to complete the proof of Lemma 5.19.

Proof of Lemma 5.19. Step 1. Setup. Suppose that we have the setup described just after the statement of Lemma 5.19. Fix z ∈ I n and w ∈ J n . We may assume without loss of generality that z ∈ I n and w ∈ J n for all n ∈ N . Also, for each n , we let Ψ n be a GFF on D rooted at z that generates Γ ′ n (see Remark 5.13). That is to say, if ϕ n : H → D is a conformal transformation mapping 0 to z , ∞ to w , and the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) arc of ∂ D to R - (resp. R + ), then the boundary conditions of Ψ n ◦ ϕ n - χ n arg( ϕ ′ n ) are given by λ ′ n on ( -∞ , 0) and λ ′ n - 2 πχ n on (0 , ∞ ), where λ ′ n def = π √ κ n / 4 and χ n def = 2 / √ κ n - √ κ n / 2. Moreover, the branching tree of counterflow lines of Ψ n centered at z and targeted at any u ∈ D is used to construct Γ ′ n as in [She09, Section 4].

Step 2. Construction of the shields. By Lemma 5.20, it suffices to verify that for each sequence ε n → 0 as n → ∞ , the bad scenarios illustrated in Figure 4 occur with probability tending to zero. To show the latter, for each n , we may choose points x n 1 , . . . , x n N n and y n 1 , . . . , y n N n on the two connected components of ∂ D \ ( I n ∪ J n ), respectively, such that all of x n 1 , . . . , x n N n (resp. y n 1 , . . . , y n N n ) lie on the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) arc of ∂ D from z to w , and such that | x n j - x n k | ∧ | y n j - y n k | ≥ 6 ε n for all distinct j, k ∈ [1 , N n ] Z and N n is of order ε - 1 n as n →∞ .

Let η n j (resp. ˜ η n j ) denote the flow line of Ψ n of angle - λ ′ n /χ n (resp. 2 π - λ ′ n /χ n ) starting from x n j (resp. y n j ) and targeted at z , so that η n j (resp. ˜ η n j ) has the law of a chordal SLE κ n ( - 1; - 1) on D from x n j (resp. y n j ) to z with the force points located at ( x n j ) - and ( x n j ) + (resp. ( y n j ) - and ( y n j ) + ) (these are the zero-angle flow lines of Ψ n - λ ′ n and Ψ n +2 πχ n - λ ′ n , whose boundary values vanish near x n j , y n j ). Consider the event E n j that η n j hits B ε n ((1 - 2 ε n ) x n j ) before exiting B 2 ε n ( x n j ) (which is measurable with respect to Ψ n | B 2 εn ( x n j ) ∩ D , flow lines being local [MS16a]). Define F n j in a similar way but with y n j in place of x n j . Then, it follows from Lemmas 5.21 and 5.23 (the former applied separately to ( E n j ) j and ( F n k ) k with r = 2 ε n , s = 1 / 2) that it holds with probability tending to 1 as n →∞ that E n j ∩ F n k occurs for some j and k .

Step 3. Conclusion. Recall that counterflow lines do not cross flow lines generated by the same random field a.s. Indeed, it follows from [MS17, Theorem 1.13] that the left and right outer boundaries of counterflow lines can be described in terms of flow lines with angles π/ 2 and - π/ 2 respectively. Thus, in order for a counterflow line η ′ to cross a flow line η , we need to have that both the flow lines with angles π/ 2 and - π/ 2 forming the left and right boundaries of η ′ cross η . But this does not happen a.s. due to the flow line interaction rules (see [MS17, Theorem 1.7]). Therefore, since every segment of a loop in Γ ′ n is part of a counterflow line of Ψ n , combining with the considerations of the previous paragraph, we obtain that the bad situations illustrated in Figure 4 occur with probability tending to zero as n →∞ . Indeed, on E n j ∩ F n k the stopped flow lines join ∂ D to depth at least ε n on each of the two arcs of ∂ D \ ( I n ∪ J n ), so that a segment of a loop of Γ ′ n contained in B ε n ( ∂ D ) and joining I n to J n would have to cross one of them. □

5.3.2. CPI exploration. In this section, we will describe in a more detailed way the exploration of simple CLE κ loops introduced in the statement of Theorem 5.14, and we will state and prove two useful consequences of it (Lemmas 5.24 and 5.25).

By setting β = 1 in Corollary 5.16, we obtain that the following is true. Let κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4). Consider the following algorithm:

- One samples a BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0). Then, inside each connected component of each true loop of this BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0), one samples an independent BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2).
- Inside each connected component of each false loop of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s and BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s of the previous step, one samples an independent BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0). Then, inside each connected component of each true loop of these BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s, one samples an independent BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2). Then, iterate this step.

Then the collection of all the true loops of all the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s has the law of a non-nested CLE κ . Moreover, we note that the collection of all the outermost true loops of all the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s has the law of a non-nested CLE κ ′ .

By iterating this construction inside each loop of the non-nested CLE κ , we obtain a nested CLE κ . But, for our purpose, we will only use the non-nested CLE κ .

Recall from Remark 5.15 that, once a root on the domain boundary is chosen, all the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s and BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s above may be coupled with a GFF Ψ with boundary conditions given by λ ′ on ( -∞ , 0) and λ ′ - 2 πχ on (0 , ∞ ) (after a conformal transformation) as flow lines and counterflow lines, respectively.

CPI exploration . Let us recall the exploration described in Theorem 5.14. Suppose that we start with the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) iteration on D rooted at some point x ∈ ∂ D . Then, we can define a path η as follows. First, we let η ′ denote the path that traces the domain boundary in a counterclockwise way except that each time it encounters a true loop of the first BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) used in the above construction, it traverses the entire loop clockwise before continuing. Let Γ denote the collection of all true loops in the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) process that we have considered inside the true loops of that BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0). Then, we define the path η by following η ′ except that each time we first encounter a point on a loop in Γ we traverse the loop in a counterclockwise way (recall that β = 1, so that all of these loops lie on the same side of η ′ ; cf. Theorem 5.14). Then, the path η traces the true loops of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s and the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s used in the first level of the iteration.

To trace the loops of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s and the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s used in the second level of the iteration, we construct a branching collection of exploration paths as follows. Each time the path η fi nishes tracing a false loop at some point y of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) or one of the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s used in the first level of the iteration, we construct a path inside the domain surrounded by that loop in the exact same way that we constructed η except that the root of the new loop is given by y . Then, we iterate the above construction inside the false loops of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s and the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s used in the second level of the iteration in the exact same way as we described in the previous two steps.

We call the branching collection of paths described in the previous two paragraphs the CPI (conformal percolation interface) exploration of [MSW17] of the iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction.

Obtaining the entire iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) / BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction from the CPI exploration . For all z ∈ ∂ D \ { x } , we let η z denote the branch of the CPI exploration targeted at z and parameterized by capacity as seen from z . Then, the collection of the true loops of the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s used in the first level of iteration will be given by the loops attached to the paths η z 's for z ∈ ∂ D \ { x } . Moreover, the boundary branching exploration tree used to construct the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) in the first level of iteration will be given by the tree formed by the trunks of the η z 's for z ∈ ∂ D \{ x } . This gives the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) and the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s used in the first level of iteration. By iterating the same procedure using the branching property of the CPI exploration inside the false loops of the above BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s and BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s, we obtain the rest of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) and BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s used in the next levels of iteration.

Re-rooting the iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) / BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction . Let us now explain how to re-root the above BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) iteration. Fix y ∈ ∂ D \ { x } and re-parameterize the path η defined above so that it starts and ends at y . Then, inside each connected component U of D \ η where there is branching of η , we re-parameterize the path that discovers the true loops of the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) in the second level of iteration which is contained in U , so that the path starts and ends at the last point on ∂U hit by η . Then, we iterate the above in the remaining connected components to obtain a branching exploration tree of paths. Hence, we can construct an iterated process of loops as explained in the previous paragraph by using the aforementioned exploration tree.

It follows from the results in [MSW17] that the iterated process of loops described in the above paragraph has the same law as the iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) described above, and the collection of true loops in the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s in the original iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction (before re-rooting) is the same as the true loops in the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s in the new iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction (after re-rooting).

Next, we state and prove the following useful Markovian property of the CPI exploration.

Lemma 5.24. Fix z ∈ D and let η z denote the branch of the CPI exploration targeted at z of an iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) / BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction. Let also τ be a stopping time for η z such that η z ( τ ) is not on a simple loop a.s. Let D τ denote the connected component of D \ η z ([0 , τ ]) containing z . Then, we have that the iterated process of loops in D τ obtained from the restriction to D τ of the CPI exploration tree rooted at η z ( τ ) has the law of an iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) / BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction on D τ conditionally on η z | [0 ,τ ] . Moreover, the simple true loops obtained from the new construction are the same as the simple loops of the original BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) / BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction which are contained in D τ .

Proof. Let Ψ denote the GFF on D used to generate the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s and the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s in the statement of the lemma. It follows from Section 5.2 and especially the proof of Theorem 5.14

(cf. [MSW17, Figure 9.2]) that the boundary conditions of Ψ on D τ are, up to an additive constant c (the constant by which Ψ is shifted at the level of the iteration being explored at time τ , cf. Remark 5.15), given by λ ′ on ( -∞ , 0) and λ ′ - 2 πχ on (0 , ∞ ) (after a conformal transformation onto H ) (by the strong Markov property of Ψ at τ , η z being a local set, and the boundary values of Ψ along flow and counterflow lines [MS16a, MS17]). Furthermore, the iterated construction of loops in D τ described in the statement of the lemma can be obtained as an iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction on D τ using the field Ψ | D τ - c . This proves the first claim in the statement of the lemma. Finally, since the simple loops in the latter construction are constructed using branching flow lines of Ψ | D τ (which are also parts of branching flow lines of Ψ) and no flow line of Ψ can cross ∂D τ (due to the flow line interaction rules, see, e.g., [MS17, Theorem 1.7]), we obtain the second claim of the lemma (both inclusions, since by the description given below the loops of the iterations inside the first level BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) of D τ are exactly the original ones). This completes the proof of the lemma. □

Let us now give a more detailed description of the relation between the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) iteration in the remaining domain and the original BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) iteration in the context of Lemma 5.24.

The relation between the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) iteration in the remaining domain and the original BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) iteration can be described as follows (see Figures 5 and 6 for an illustration): Let x be a point that is on the boundary of the remaining domain. Since we always finish tracing the entire loop at the first time we encounter a true loop of some BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2), and η z ( τ ) is not on a loop of some BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2), we see that x is either

- on the false BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) loop that surrounds the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ (by convention, the domain boundary is also viewed as a false BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) loop);
- or a false loop of the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) that is on the right-hand side of η z ( τ ) (note that η z ( τ ) is on both a true and a false loop of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ ; the true one is on the right-hand side of η z ( τ ); the false one is on the left);
- or the false BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop that we were tracing at time τ which is on the left-hand side of η z ( τ ).

Then the branch of the first level BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) in the remaining domain from η z ( τ ) to x can be described as follows:

- in the first case, we follow the branch of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ targeting x ;
- in the second case, the root of the false BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) loop that contains x is on the true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop that we were tracing at time τ and is to the right of η z ( τ ); then we follow this true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop until the root of the false BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) loop that contains x , then we trace the branch of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) inside this false BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) loop from its root to x ;
- in the third case, we follow the false BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop to the left of η z ( τ ) until we close it, then we follow the branch of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) inside this false BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop targeting x .

Once this first level BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) is constructed, by the description above, we observe that each connected component of each true (resp. false) loop of this BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) is exactly the connected component of some true (resp. false) loop of some original BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0). Thus, the second level BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) and the third level BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0), and so on, inside them, are exactly given by the original ones inside them.

x

The light green depicts the remainder of this true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop. The orange loops are two false BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) loops inside this true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop. In this case, the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) branch in the remaining domain from η z ( τ ) to x is given by following the branch of the original BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ targeting x .

Note that the loops of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s in the remaining domain are not necessarily loops of the original BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s. However, we have the following.

Lemma 5.25. Suppose that we have the same setup as in Lemma 5.24. Recall that the set of outermost true loops of all the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) 's has the law of a non-nested CLE κ ′ . Let ℐ be a connected arc of some loop of this non-nested CLE κ ′ . Suppose that ℐ is contained in D τ (hence it does not intersect η z ([0 , τ ]) ). Then ℐ is contained in some loop of the non-nested CLE κ ′ associated with the iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) / BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction in D τ .

Proof. Step 1. The possible positions of ℐ . Consider the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ . Since ℐ is contained in the remaining domain and ℐ is assumed to be contained in an outermost true loop of some BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0), it follows that this BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ is not surrounded by some other true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop. Moreover, since ℐ must be contained in a outermost true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop, it cannot be contained in any true or false loop of any BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) inside the true loops of the current BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ . Thus, either

Figure 6. Left: The second case of x , where x is on a false loop of the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) inside the true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop that is on the right-hand side of η z ( τ ). In this case, the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) branch in the remaining domain from η z ( τ ) to x is given by following the true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop that is on the right-hand side of η z ( τ ) until closing the false BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) loop that contains x , and then follow the branch of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) inside this false BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) loop from its root targeting x . Right: The third case of x , where x is on the false BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop that we were tracing at time τ which is on the left-hand side of η z ( τ ). In this case, the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) branch in the remaining domain from η z ( τ ) to x is given by following the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ until we close this false BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop, then we follow the branch of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) inside this false BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop targeting x .

<!-- image -->

- ℐ is contained in the true loop of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ , or
- ℐ is contained in the false loop of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ (in a true loop of the outermost BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) inside it, or deeper in the iteration), or

Figure 7. Illustration of one situation of the proof of Lemma 5.25, where ℐ is contained in the false BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop that lies immediately to the left of η z ( τ ). The connected arc ℐ is depicted in green. Left: The original loop that contains ℐ (the union of the red and green arcs) touches η z | [0 ,τ ] , in which case this loop is again a true loop of the first level BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) in the remaining domain. Right: The original loop that contains ℐ does not touch η z | [0 ,τ ] . Then the light blue region is a connected component of a false loop of the first level BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) in the remaining domain, and the original loop that contains ℐ is a true loop of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) inside it.

<!-- image -->

- ℐ is outside of the true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ and outside of the false loop of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ .

Consider the domain that contains the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ . (We may assume without loss of generality that this is the initial domain.)

Step 2. The first case. Consider the first case above. In this case, ℐ must be part of the branch of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ from η z ( τ ) and targeting a point that is both on the domain boundary and the boundary of the remaining domain. Moreover, by the previous discussions, this branch must also be a branch of some outermost true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop in the remaining domain (cf. Figure 6 (left)). Since a branch of a BCLE can only separate into distinct loops at the boundary (every loop of a BCLE being a pocket cut out between two consecutive boundary hits of a branch), and ℐ does not touch the boundary of the remaining domain, it follows that ℐ is contained in some true loop of the outermost BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) in the remaining domain.

Step 3. The false loop to the left of η z ( τ ) . Next, we consider the case that ℐ is contained in the false loop that is immediately to the left of η z ( τ ). See Figure 7 for an illustration of this case. Note that it is possible that ℐ touches the false loop that is immediately to the left of η z ( τ ) (but not the portion of it that is contained in η z | [0 ,τ ] ). Note that there are again two subcases: either the original CLE κ ′ loop that contains ℐ touches η z ( τ ) (cf. Figure 7 (left)) or not (cf. Figure 7 (right)). In the first subcase, by the previous discussions (cf. Figure 6 (right)), this loop is a true loop of the outermost BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) in the remaining domain. In the second subcase, it is a true loop of some BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) inside a false loop of the outermost BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) in the remaining domain.

Step 4. The remaining cases. Finally, we observe that, for the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that we were tracing at time τ , except for the true loop that is immediately to the right of η z ( τ ) and the false loop that is immediately to the left of η z ( τ ), all other loops (true or false) of this BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) that are contained in the remaining domain are also loops of the outermost BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) in the remaining domain, and the iteration constructions inside them are exactly the same for the original domain and for the remaining domain. Thus, except for the cases discussed in the preceding two paragraphs, other cases of ℐ follow by this observation. This completes the proof of Lemma 5.25. □

- 5.3.3. The exploration gives all the loops. As explained at the beginning of Section 5.3, one of the main ingredients of the proof of Proposition 5.2 is a certain Markovian exploration of the loops of a simple (non-nested) CLE κ in D that intersect the inner and outer boundaries of a fixed Euclidean annulus. In this subsection, we are going to describe the aforementioned exploration in a detailed way and prove that it discovers all the simple loops intersecting the inner and outer boundaries of the annulus a.s. (Lemma 5.26). We will also prove the following, for each fixed κ ′ (Lemma 5.29). Every segment of a loop in the CLE κ ′ in D (which is coupled with the simple CLE κ as in Theorem 5.14) that is included in one of the conformal rectangles formed by the crossings of the annulus by CLE κ loops (on the event that such crossings exist) is part of a loop of a CLE κ ′ in the above conformal rectangle.

We continue to use the notation of the preceding subsection. Let Γ denote the non-nested CLE κ consisting of the true loops of all the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s.

In the present subsection, we fix an annulus A s,t ( z ) = B t ( z ) \ B s ( z ) inside D . On the event that there exists a loop of Γ crossing between the inner and outer boundaries of A s,t ( z ), we define an exploration that explores all the loops of Γ that intersect the boundary of A s,t ( z ), and such that the restriction of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s and BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s in the remaining domains again has the law of the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) iteration construction in the remaining domains.

Recall that the collection of all the outermost true loops of all the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)'s forms a non-nested CLE κ ′ . Let us denote it by Γ ′ .

More precisely, the exploration is defined in the following order. Let ( Z n ) n ≥ 1 be a sequence of i.i.d. uniform random variables on D . Then the exploration is defined inductively. We start with the iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction used to generate Γ and Γ ′ , and re-root it (in the sense of Section 5.3.2) at some point w 1 chosen (independently of everything else) according to the harmonic measure seen from Z 1 in ∂ D \ A s,t ( z ) (which is actually ∂ D since A s,t ( z ) ⊂ D ). Then, we consider the branch of the CPI exploration associated with the iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction re-rooted at w 1 , started at w 1 and targeted at Z 1 , and we stop it at the first time that it discovers a loop in Γ that intersects A s,t ( z ) , or when it hits A s,t ( z ) (but it might be the case that the branch of the CPI exploration is not stopped, in which case we draw the full branch to Z 1 ).

Assume that the exploration is constructed until step n ≥ 1. The unexplored region at step n is defined as the connected component containing Z n +1 of the complement in D of the union of all branches drawn during the first n steps of the exploration and of the interiors of the CLE κ loops discovered in the first n steps of the exploration. Recall that Lemma 5.24 implies that the iterated process of loops constructed using the restriction to the unexplored region at step n of the original CPI exploration in D has the law of an iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction in the unexplored region.

On the event that Z n +1 does not belong to A s,t ( z ) and that Z n +1 is in the unexplored region, we sample a point w n +1 (independently of everything else) according to the harmonic measure seen from Z n +1 on the subset of the boundary of the unexplored region which is not included in A s,t ( z ). Then, we consider the branch of the CPI exploration associated with the iterated BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction in the unexplored region re-rooted at w n +1 , started at w n +1 and targeted at Z n +1 , and stopped at the first time that it discovers a loop in Γ intersecting A s,t ( z ), or when it hits A s,t ( z ).

On the event that Z n +1 belongs to A s,t ( z ) or is not in the unexplored region, we do nothing.

Note that Lemma 5.24 implies that all the simple true loops discovered during the steps of the above exploration are loops in Γ. Also, for each n , the conditional law of the path that we draw at step n (on the event that Z n does not belong to A s,t ( z ) and is in the unexplored region) given all the paths that we have drawn during the first n - 1 steps is that of a radial SLE κ ( κ - 6) with β = 1 in the unexplored region at step n , by Lemma 5.24 together with Theorem 5.14.

Let us check that the above exploration indeed discovers all the loops intersecting the boundary of A s,t ( z ).

Lemma 5.26. Almost surely, the above exploration indeed discovers all the loops of Γ intersecting the outer boundary of A s,t ( z ) . The same is true for the inner boundary on the event that there is a CLE κ loop which crosses A s,t ( z ) .

Proof. Step 1. Reduction to hitting a fixed ball. Let us first deal with the loops intersecting the outer boundary of A s,t ( z ). Let z ′ ∈ D \ A s,t ( z ) be a point that is not disconnected from ∂ D by A s,t ( z ) and let ε &gt; 0. It suffices to show that the loop ℒ of Γ surrounding B ε ( z ′ ) (when it exists) will be a.s. discovered by the above exploration (a.s. no loop of Γ is tangent to ∂B s ( z ) or ∂B t ( z ), so every loop meeting ∂A s,t ( z ) has interior points outside A s,t ( z )). Let δ ∈ (0 , ε ) such that B 2 δ ( z ′ ) ∩ A s,t ( z ) = ∅ .

Step 2. Construction of a corridor of definite width. For all j ≥ 1, let n j be the j -th integer n such that Z n ∈ B δ ( z ′ ). Let U j denote the connected component of the unexplored region at step n j - 1 containing Z n j . Let f j : U j → D be the conformal map from the connected component of the unexplored region containing Z n j to D such that f j ( Z n j ) = 0 and f ′ j ( Z n j ) &gt; 0. Note that since Z n j ∈ B δ ( z ′ ) and B 2 δ ( z ′ ) ∩ A s,t ( z ) = ∅ , the probability that a planar Brownian motion starting from Z n j hits ∂ D before A s,t ( z ) is bounded from below by a constant p &gt; 0 which does not depend on j . In particular, the probability that a planar Brownian motion starting from Z n j hits the explored region before hitting A s,t ( z ) is bounded from below by p . Therefore, the probability that a planar Brownian motion starting from 0 hits ∂ D before f j ( A s,t ( z ) ∩ U j ) is bounded from below by p . Moreover, note that the number of connected components of f j ( A s,t ( z ) ∩ U j ) is at most one plus the number of CLE κ loops crossing A s,t ( z ), so that the number of connected components of f j ( A s,t ( z ) ∩ U j ) is bounded by a random variable N which does not depend on j . Thus, on the event that N ≤ m , for some fixed and deterministic m ∈ N , there exists ε ′ ∈ (0 , δ ) depending only on p, δ , and m which does not depend on j , and a path γ j from ∂ D to 0 such that B 2 ε ′ ( γ j ) ⊂ D \ f j ( A s,t ( z ) ∩ U j ) (by Beurling's estimate: if every path from 0 to ∂ D in this complement had a bottleneck of width smaller than ε ′ , then, the complement having at most m +1 boundary components, the harmonic measure of ∂ D from 0 in it would be smaller than p ). For any path γ in D , there exists a path ˜ γ in the grid ( ε ′ / 8) Z 2 which is ε ′ / 2-close to γ for the Hausdorff distance so actually we may divide ε ′ by two to assume that γ j is the intersection with D of a path in the grid ( ε ′ / 8) Z 2 . Note that there are finitely many such simple paths.

Step 3. The trunk follows the corridor. Next, we apply [MW17, Lemma 2.3] to the trunk of a radial SLE κ ( κ - 6) starting from a random point on ∂ D ∩ B ε ′ ( γ j ) sampled according to the Lebesgue measure and independently of everything else, and targeting 0. The trunk is a radial SLE κ ′ (0; κ ′ - 6) targeting 0 by [MSW22, Theorem 1.6] and one can see that κ ′ - 6 &gt; - 2. So, by [MW17, Lemma 2.3], with probability at least p 0 ( ε ′ ) &gt; 0, where p 0 ( ε ′ ) is deterministic and only depends on ε ′ , the trunk η of a radial SLE κ ( κ - 6) starting from a random point on ∂ D ∩ B ε ′ ( γ j ) sampled according to the Lebesgue measure and independently of everything else, and targeting 0, stopped when it hits f j ( B δ ( z ′ )), is included in B ε ′ ( γ j ). Note that on this event the trunk stopped when it hits f j ( B δ ( z ′ )) can be coupled with one chordal SLE κ ′ ( κ ′ - 6) targeting a fixed point in ∂ D \ B ε ′ ( γ j ) [SW05] so that we only apply [MW17, Lemma 2.3] to one chordal curve. The fact that p 0 ( ε ′ ) does not depend on the choice of the path γ j stems from the fact that there are finitely many paths in the grid ( ε ′ / 8) Z 2 that are included in the unit disk.

Step 4. The attached loops stay near the trunk. Recall that Theorem 5.14 implies that the radial SLE κ ( κ - 6) is obtained by attaching BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) loops on the right-hand side of the trunk. These loops are traced by a chordal SLE κ ( - κ/ 2; 3 κ/ 2 - 6) from the right-hand side of the starting point of the trunk to the tip of the stopped trunk η . As in the previous paragraph, we conformally map the unexplored component containing Z n j to the unit disk and Z n j is sent to 0 and we denote by ˜ η the image of the chordal SLE κ ( - κ/ 2; 3 κ/ 2 - 6). Recall that η is the trunk stopped when it hits f j ( B δ ( z ′ )) for the first time. So, conditionally on η , the curve ˜ η is a chordal SLE κ ( - κ/ 2; 3 κ/ 2 - 6) from the right-hand side of η (0) to the endpoint of η , where its force points are located immediately to the left and right of η (0) respectively [MSW17].

Let V j denote the connected component of D \ η containing 0, and let g j : V j → H be the conformal transformation mapping V j onto H such that it maps the rightmost point of η ∩ ∂ D to 0, the tip of η to 1, the leftmost point of η ∩ ∂ D to ∞ , and the right-hand side of η onto [0 , 1]. Then, g j ( ˜ η ) is a chordal SLE κ ( - κ/ 2; 3 κ/ 2 - 6) from 0 to 1 with the force points located at 0 - and 0 + respectively. Furthermore, note that there exists some deterministic constant c ( ε ′ , δ ) &gt; 0 depending only on ε ′ and δ such that with probability at least c ( ε ′ , δ ) &gt; 0, a planar Brownian motion starting from 0 stopped when it hits ∂ D for the first time stays in the union B δ (0) ∪ B R ε ′ ( η ), where B R ε ′ ( η ) is the set of points of D at distance smaller than ε ′ from the right-hand side of η (and the same is true if we replace the right-hand side of η by the left-hand side of η ). In particular, there exists ε ′′ &gt; 0 depending only on ε ′ , δ such that, noting that [0 , 1] is the image by g j of the right-hand side of η , we have

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Moreover, [GMQ21, Lemma A.1] implies that there exists some deterministic constant p 1 ( ε ′′ ) &gt; 0 depending only on ε ′′ such that a.s., with conditional probability at least p 1 ( ε ′′ ), we have that g j ( ˜ η ) ⊆ B ε ′′ ([0 , 1]). In particular, going back to the unit disk, we have that a.s., with conditional probability at least p 1 ( ε ′′ ), we have that ˜ η does not hit f j ( A s,t ( z ) ∩ U j ).

Step 5. Conclusion for the outer boundary. Thus, conditionally on the explored region at step n j - 1, with probability at least p ′ &gt; 0 for some p ′ &gt; 0 deterministic which does not depend on j ≥ 1, the radial SLE κ ( κ - 6) will hit B δ ( z ′ ). So, a.s., the exploration will hit B δ ( z ′ ) in finitely many steps . In particular, a.s., the exploration will discover the loop ℒ when it exists.

Step 6. The inner boundary. Finally, let us deal with the loops intersecting the inner boundary of A s,t ( z ) on the event that there is a CLE κ loop crossing A s,t ( z ). Let z ′ be in the connected component of D \ A s,t ( z ) which is disconnected from ∂ D . Let δ &gt; 0 such that B 2 δ ( z ′ ) ∩ A s,t ( z ) = ∅ . On the event that there is a CLE κ loop crossing A s,t ( z ), we have already shown that such a loop will be explored a.s. Let n 0 be the step at which the first loop crossing A s,t ( z ) is discovered. Then, note that for all n &gt; n 0 such that Z n ∈ B δ ( z ′ ), the probability that a planar Brownian motion starting from Z n hits the explored region before A s,t ( z ) is bounded from below by some (random) constant p ′′ &gt; 0 which does not depend on n . We can then apply the same reasoning as in Steps 2 to 5. □

Recall that, on the event that there is a loop of Γ crossing the annulus, the loops of Γ intersecting the boundary of A s,t ( z ) bound a finite number of conformal rectangles. Each rectangle is a connected component of A s,t ( z ) minus the closure of the union of the domains encircled by the loops of Γ which intersect the boundary of A s,t ( z ). Let us also check that the connected component of the unexplored region at step n of the exploration defined at the beginning of the current subsection containing the conformal rectangle converges to that conformal rectangle. More precisely, let R 1 , . . . , R k be the conformal rectangles bounded by the loops of Γ intersecting ∂A s,t ( z ). Let R n 1 , . . . , R n k be the connected components of the unexplored region at step n of the exploration containing respectively R 1 , . . . , R k (note that these connected components are not necessarily distinct).

Lemma 5.27. On the event that there is a loop of Γ crossing A s,t ( z ) , a.s., for each conformal rectangle bounded by the loops of Γ intersecting ∂A s,t ( z ) , the connected component of the unexplored region at step n converges as n →∞ in the Carathéodory sense to this rectangle.

Proof. Let j ∈ [1 , k ] Z . By Lemma 5.26, we know that for any loop of Γ intersecting the boundary of A s,t ( z ) there exists n ≥ 1 such that the loop has been discovered at step n .

Let K ⊂ R j be a compact subset, then we automatically have K ⊂ R n j for all n ≥ 1 since R j ⊆ R n j by construction (the branches are stopped upon hitting A s,t ( z ), and the interiors of the discovered loops do not meet the rectangles).

Let w ∈ R j . Let U ∋ w be a connected open subset of D such that U ⊆ R n j for infinitely many n ≥ 1. Let us show that U ⊆ R j . Let ℒ be a loop of Γ which intersects U and intersects ∂A s,t ( z ), then ℒ will be discovered eventually by the exploration by Lemma 5.26, which is impossible since U ⊆ R n j for infinitely many n ≥ 1. Therefore, the loops of Γ that intersect U are either contained in A s,t ( z ) or in D \ A s,t ( z ). Therefore, since U is open and connected, we see that U is included in D minus the closure of the union of the loops of Γ which intersect ∂A s,t ( z ). Thus, by definition of R j and by connectedness of U , we deduce that U ⊆ R j (here we use that a.s. every point of ∂A s,t ( z ) lies in the closure of the union of the domains encircled by the loops of Γ meeting ∂A s,t ( z ), so that U cannot leave A s,t ( z )). □

From the above lemma, we get in particular the convergence of the CLE κ ′ 's in the connected components of the unexplored regions R n j containing the conformal rectangles R n j for j ∈ [1 , k ] Z toward CLE κ ′ 's in the R j 's.

Lemma 5.28. On the event that there is a loop of Γ crossing A s,t ( z ) and that there are k ≥ 1 conformal rectangles R 1 , . . . , R k , for all j ∈ [1 , k ] Z , let Γ ′ R n j be the non-nested CLE κ ′ associated with the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) / BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) iteration construction (see Corollary 5.16) in the connected component of the unexplored region R n j containing R j . For all x ∈ R j ∩ Q 2 , consider the loop ℒ n j ( x ) of Γ ′ R n j surrounding x . Then, for all simply connected open subset U ⊂ R j such that U ⊂ R j there exists a sequence ( U n ) n ≥ 1 of simply connected open subsets such that U n → U a.s. in the Hausdorff topology, U n → U a.s. in the Carathéodory sense, and such that the conditional laws given R n j of the U n ∩ ℒ n j ( x ) 's for x ∈ R j ∩ Q 2 converge a.s. weakly, in the Hausdorff topology, to the conditional law given R j of the U ∩ ℒ j ( x ) 's for x ∈ R j ∩ Q 2 , where the ℒ j ( x ) 's are the loops of a CLE κ ′ in R j .

Proof. The Carathéodory convergence of Lemma 5.27 can be written as the almost sure uniform convergence on compact subsets of the conformal mapping f n j : D → R n j such that f n j (0) = z j and ( f n j ) ′ (0) &gt; 0 to the conformal mapping f j : D → R j such that f j (0) = z j and f ′ j (0) &gt; 0, where z j is a point of R j chosen arbitrarily in a deterministic way. Combining this convergence with the fact that ( f n j ) - 1 (Γ ′ R n j ) is a CLE κ ′ in the unit disk (the unexplored components being simply connected), we get the desired result, taking U n = f n j ( f - 1 j ( U )). Let z ′ j ∈ U . Taking a conformal mapping ϕ : D → f - 1 j ( U ) such that ( ϕ ◦ f j )(0) = z ′ j and ( ϕ ◦ f j ) ′ (0) &gt; 0 we see that ϕ ◦ f n j converges a.s. to ϕ ◦ f j uniformly on compact subsets of D so that U n → U almost surely in the Carathéodory sense. □

Combining the above lemma with Lemma 5.25, we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 5.29. We work on the event that there is a loop of Γ crossing A s,t ( z ) . Recall that we denote by R 1 , . . . , R k be the conformal rectangles bounded by the loops of Γ intersecting ∂A s,t ( z ) . Then, there is a coupling with loop ensembles Γ ′ R 1 , . . . , Γ ′ R k in the respective conformal rectangles R 1 , . . . , R k that are CLE κ ′ 's conditionally on R 1 , . . . , R k and such that the following holds almost surely. For all segment ℐ of a loop of Γ ′ which is included a conformal rectangle R j for j ∈ [1 , k ] Z , ℐ is a segment of a loop of Γ ′ R j .

Proof. Let j ∈ [1 , k ] Z . Let U ⊂ R j by a simply connected open subset such that U ⊂ R j . By Lemma 5.28, we know that there exists a sequence of simply connected open subsets U n ⊂ R n j such that U n → U a.s. in the Carathéodory sense, such that the conditional laws given R n j of the U n ∩ ℒ n j ( x )'s for x ∈ R j ∩ Q 2 converge a.s. weakly, in the Hausdorff topology, to the conditional law given R j of the U ∩ ℒ j ( x )'s for x ∈ R j ∩ Q 2 , where the ℒ j ( x )'s are the loops of a CLE κ ′ in R j that we denote by Γ ′ R j .

Furthermore let ℐ 1 , . . . , ℐ r be segments of loops of Γ ′ that are included in U . For all n ≥ 1, by Lemma 5.25, applied inductively at the first n steps, using the notation of Lemma 5.28, we know that ℐ i is a segment of a loop of Γ ′ R n j for all i ∈ [1 , r ] Z . By taking the first part of the proof into account, we see that there is a coupling with Γ ′ R j such that for all i ∈ [1 , r ] Z , the segment ℐ i is a segment of a loop of Γ ′ R j . □

5.4. Completion of the proof of Proposition 5.2. In this last subsection, Γ n is a (non-nested) CLE κ n and Γ ′ n is a (non-nested) CLE κ ′ n . We would like to combine Proposition 5.3 with Lemmas 5.19 and 5.29 to complete the proof of Proposition 5.2. However, there are two main issues remaining. First, we must ensure that whenever a loop in Γ ′ n intersects a loop in Γ n , it actually surrounds it, so that we can use Proposition 5.3. Second, Lemma 5.29 requires the annulus A s,t ( z ) to be strictly contained in D , which means we must control the behavior of loops near ∂ D . To resolve these issues, we proceed in three steps:

- (i) In Section 5.4.1, we show that, under the setup of Sections 5.3.2 to 5.3.3, whenever a loop in Γ ′ n intersects a loop in Γ n , it necessarily surrounds it.
- (ii) In Section 5.4.2, we establish that with probability tending to 1 as n → ∞ , macroscopic loops in Γ ′ n do not intersect ∂ D .
- (iii) In Section 5.4.3, we combine these ingredients to complete the proof of Proposition 5.2.

5.4.1. Every loop in the non-simple CLE that intersects a loop in the simple CLE has to surround it. Fix κ ′ ∈ (4 , 8) and let Γ ′ (resp. Γ) denote a CLE κ ′ (resp. CLE κ ) in D coupled as in Sections 5.3.2 to 5.3.3 with κ = 16 /κ ′ . We will prove the following (thereby resolving one of the two issues described above).

Lemma 5.30. It is a.s. the case that the following holds. If ℒ ′ is a loop in Γ ′ that intersects some loop ℒ in Γ , then ℒ ′ surrounds ℒ .

The main ingredient in the proof of Lemma 5.30 is the following lemma which says that any two distinct loops of any CLE κ ′ for κ ′ ∈ (4 , 8) a.s. do not intersect at points which are cut points of either of the loops, where a point x on a loop ℒ is called a cut point of ℒ if x lies on the intersection of the outer boundary of ℒ (i.e., the boundary of the unbounded connected component of C \ ℒ ) with the boundary of some connected component of C \ ℒ which is surrounded by ℒ .

̸

Lemma 5.31. Let Γ ′ be a CLE κ ′ in D with κ ′ ∈ (4 , 8) . Then, it is a.s. the case that the following is true. Let ℒ , ℒ ′ be two distinct loops in Γ ′ such that ℒ ∩ ℒ ′ = ∅ . Then, ℒ ∩ ℒ ′ does not contain any cut points of ℒ or of ℒ ′ .

Lemma 5.31 will follow from combining Lemmas 5.32 and 5.33 below. Lemma 5.32 states that the complementary connected components of a chordal SLE κ ′ ( κ ′ - 6) in D that are surrounded by the curve do not intersect ∂ D a.s. Lemma 5.33 states that the loops in a CLE κ ′ in D do not have cut points on ∂ D a.s.

Lemma 5.32. Fix κ ′ ∈ (4 , 8) and let η ′ be an SLE κ ′ ( κ ′ - 6) curve in D from - i to i with the force point located at ( - i) + . Then, it is a.s. the case that if U is any connected component of D \ η ′ such that ∂U ⊆ η ′ , we have that ∂U ∩ ∂ D = ∅ .

Proof. Fix z ∈ D Q and let U denote the connected component of D \ η ′ containing z . For the rest of the proof, we will assume that we are working on the event that ∂U ⊆ η ′ . Suppose first that η ′ draws ∂U in the clockwise way. Then, it was shown in Step 2 in the proof of [DM25, Proposition 3.1] that it is a.s. the case that ∂U does not intersect the clockwise arc of ∂ D from - i to i.

We will show that ∂U does not intersect the counterclockwise arc of ∂ D from - i to i and hence conclude that ∂U ∩ ∂ D = ∅ . Suppose that this does not hold and let t 1 (resp. t 2 ) be the first (resp. last) time that η ′ intersects ∂U and note that w = η ′ ( t 1 ) = η ′ ( t 2 ). Let s ∈ [ t 1 , t 2 ] denote the last time before t 2 that η ′ intersects the counterclockwise arc of ∂ D from - i to i. Note that it is explained in the proof of [She09, Theorem 5.4] that it is a.s. the case that SLE κ ′ hits each point on the boundary of the domain that it is defined at most once. Thus, since an SLE κ ′ ( κ ′ - 6) curve targeted at its force point has the law of an SLE κ ′ curve (and, by target invariance [She09], η ′ agrees with the branch targeted at the force point up to the time at which the target points are disconnected), we obtain that it is a.s. the case that η ′ hits each point on ∂ D at most once. In particular, we have that t 1 &lt; s &lt; t 2 and w / ∈ ∂ D .

Let τ be the last time before t 1 that η ′ intersects the counterclockwise arc of ∂ D from - i to i and let V denote the connected component of D \ η ′ ([0 , s ]) whose boundary contains the counterclockwise arc of ∂ D from η ′ ( τ ) to η ′ ( s ). Then, since ∂U is drawn by η ′ in the clockwise way and η ′ does not trace itself and hits w after time s , we obtain that there exists t ∈ ( s, t 2 ) such that η ′ ( t ) ∈ V . In particular, we have that ∂V disconnects η ′ ( t ) from i but this contradicts the fact that η ′ does not cross itself a.s. Therefore, we obtain that ∂U ∩ ∂ D = ∅ on the event that η ′ draws ∂U in the clockwise way.

A similar argument shows that ∂U ∩ ∂ D = ∅ a.s. on the event that η ′ draws ∂U in the counterclockwise way. Therefore, this completes the proof of the lemma since z ∈ D Q was arbitrary and η ′ does not hit fixed points a.s. □

Lemma 5.33. Let Γ ′ be a CLE κ ′ in D with κ ′ ∈ (4 , 8) . Then, a.s., the loops of Γ ′ do not have any cut points on ∂ D .

Proof. The claim in the statement of the lemma follows from Lemma 5.32 combined with the BCLE κ ′ (0) construction of the boundary touching loops of a CLE κ ′ (the branches of the BCLE κ ′ (0) being SLE κ ′ (0; κ ′ - 6) processes, i.e. the SLE κ ′ ( κ ′ - 6) processes of Lemma 5.32, a force point of weight 0 having no effect). □

̸

Proof of Lemma 5.31. Fix z, w ∈ D Q distinct points and let ℒ ( z ) (resp. ℒ ( w )) denote the loop in Γ ′ surrounding z (resp. w ). Suppose that we are working on the event that ℒ ( z ) = ℒ ( w ). Let P denote the collection of all simple polygonal paths γ : [0 , 1] → D such that γ (0) = e iθ for some θ ∈ [0 , 2 π ) ∩ Q , γ (1) = w , γ ((0 , 1)) ⊆ D , and γ | (0 , 1) consists of a finite union of segments with endpoints in D Q . Note that since ℒ ( z ) = ℒ ( w ), we have that ℒ ( z ) does not disconnect w from ∂ D and so there exists a path γ in P such that γ ∩ ℒ ( z ) = ∅ .

̸

̸

Fix γ ∈ P and suppose that we are working on the event that γ ∩ ℒ ( z ) = ∅ . Let K denote the closure of the union of γ and of the loops in Γ ′ that intersect γ and let ( V j ) j ≥ 1 denote the collection of the connected components of D \ K . Then, since K ∩ ∂ D = ∅ and K is connected (each of these loops meeting γ ), we have that V j is simply connected for all j . Moreover, the locality property of CLE κ ′ [She09, GMQ21] implies that conditioned on K , the conditional law of the restrictions of Γ ′ to the V j 's is that of independent copies of CLE κ ′ 's in each component.

Since ℒ ( z ) ∩ γ = ∅ , we have that there exists n ∈ N such that ℒ ( z ) ⊆ V n (the loop ℒ ( z ) being connected, disjoint from γ , and not crossing the loops of Γ ′ ). But then, combining the locality property of CLE with Lemma 5.33, we obtain that it is a.s. the case that there are no cut points of ℒ ( z ) which are contained in ∂V n . Therefore, since ℒ ( w ) ⊆ K and hence ℒ ( z ) ∩ ℒ ( w ) ⊆ ∂V n , we obtain that it is a.s. the case that there are no cut points of ℒ ( z ) which are contained in ℒ ( w ).

Finally, the proof of the lemma is complete since z, w ∈ D Q and γ ∈ P were arbitrary, and since for every fixed and deterministic point in D , it is a.s. the case that there exists a loop in Γ ′ surrounding it. □

Proof of Lemma 5.30. Recall that Γ ′ corresponds to the outermost true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loops in the BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0)/BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2) construction described in Theorem 5.14. In particular, if ℒ , ℒ ′ are as in the statement of the lemma, and ℒ ′ does not surround ℒ , then we have that the outermost true BCLE ⟳ κ ′ (0) loop ℒ ′′ surrounding ℒ must intersect ℒ ′ and be distinct from ℒ ′ . But then, we have that every point on ℒ ∩ ℒ ′ is a cut point in ℒ ′′ (the loops of Γ ′ do not cross each other, so ℒ ∩ ℒ ′ ⊆ ℒ ′′ ), and we have already shown in Lemma 5.31 that it is a.s. the case that there are no cut points of ℒ ′′ that are contained in ℒ ′ . Therefore, we obtain that ℒ ′ surrounds ℒ a.s. This completes the proof of the lemma. □

5.4.2. Large loops in Γ ′ n do not intersect the boundary with high probability when n is large. Next, we will prove that with probability tending to 1 as n → ∞ , there are no loops in Γ ′ n with large Euclidean diameter that intersect ∂ D (thereby resolving the second main issue mentioned at the beginning of Section 5.4). Formally, we will show the following.

Lemma 5.34. Fix ε, p ∈ (0 , 1) . Then, there exists n 0 ∈ N such that the following is true for all n ≥ n 0 . With probability at least p , we have that there is no loop in Γ ′ n intersecting ∂ D whose diameter is at least ε .

Before we prove Lemma 5.34, we state and prove two lemmas. The following lemma states that with probability tending to 1 as n → ∞ , there are no loops in Γ ′ n with large Euclidean diameter which stay close to ∂ D .

Lemma 5.35. Fix ε &gt; 0 and p ∈ (0 , 1) . Then, there exists δ ∈ (0 , 1) depending only on p and ε such that the following holds for all n ∈ N sufficiently large (the threshold depending only on ε and p ). With probability at least p , there is no loop ℒ in Γ ′ n with diameter at least ε such that ℒ ⊆ D \ B 1 - δ (0) .

<!-- image -->

Figure 8. Illustration of the proof of Lemma 5.35. The orange loop contained in the green annulus D \ B 1 - δ (0) cannot cross the flow line η n, R j,i j , so that all the points of η n, R j,i j ∩ ∂ D are hit at least twice by the loop (except if the orange loop surrounds B 1 - δ (0)) which is impossible.

Proof. Step 1. Setup and the shield events. As in the proof of Lemma 5.19, for each n , we let Ψ n be a GFF on D rooted at - i that generates Γ ′ n as in Remark 5.13. Then, the branching tree of counterflow lines of Ψ n centered at - i and targeted at any z ∈ D is used to construct Γ ′ n .

We partition the counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) arc of ∂ D from - i to i into arcs I R 1 , . . . , I R m ε (resp. I L 1 , . . . , I L m ε ) of length at most ε/ 2, where m ε ∈ N depends only on ε . Fix δ ∈ (0 , ε ) sufficiently small (depending only on p and ε ) (only the smallness of δ is used, both here and at the end of the proof). By shrinking δ if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that ε/ (10 δ ) ∈ N . For each j ∈ { 1 , . . . , m ε } , we choose points x R j, 1 , . . . , x R j,k δ (resp. x L j, 1 , . . . , x L j,k δ ) on I R j (resp. I L j ) ordered in the counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) way such that for all m ∈ { 1 , . . . , k δ - 1 } , the counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) arc of ∂ D from x R j,m to x R j,m +1 (resp. from x L j,m to x L j,m +1 ) has length equal to 5 δ , where k δ = ε/ (10 δ ) - 1 ∈ N . Also, the counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) arcs of I R j (resp. I L j ) between one endpoint of I R j (resp. I L j ) and x R j, 1 (resp. x L j, 1 ), and the other endpoint of I R j (resp. I L j ) and x R j,k δ (resp. x L j,k δ ) both have length 5 δ .

Furthermore, for all k ∈ { 1 , . . . , k δ } , we let η n, R j,k (resp. η n, L j,k ) denote the flow line of Ψ n of angle 2 π - λ ′ n /χ n (resp. - λ ′ n /χ n ) starting from x R j,k (resp. x L j,k ) and targeted at - i, where we recall that λ ′ n = π √ κ n / 4 and χ n = 2 / √ κ n - √ κ n / 2. Also, for q ∈ { L , R } and k ∈ { 1 , . . . , k δ } , we let E n,q j,k denote the event that η n,q j,k hits B δ ((1 - 2 δ ) x q j,k ) before exiting B 2 δ ( x q j,k ), and note that Lemma 5.23 implies that there exists some universal constant ˜ p ∈ (0 , 1) such that P [ E n,q j,k ] ≥ ˜ p .

Step 2. Many shields occur. Therefore, Lemma 5.21 implies that we can choose δ ∈ (0 , 1) sufficiently small (in a way that depends only on p and ε ) such that for all n ∈ N , the following holds with probability at least p . For all j ∈ { 1 , . . . , m ε } , there exist i L j , i R j ∈ { 1 , . . . , k δ } such that E n, L j,i L j ∩ E n, R j,i R j occurs. Suppose that we are working on that event and let ℒ be a loop in Γ ′ n with diameter at least ε such that ℒ ⊆ D \ B 1 - δ (0). Then, there exist j ∈ { 1 , . . . , m ε } , q ∈ { L , R } such that ℒ makes a crossing between the left and right boundaries of the conformal rectangle bounded by I q j , ∂B 1 - δ (0), and the two segments connecting the two endpoints of I q j to ∂B 1 - δ (0) (indeed, ℒ joins two points at distance at least ε while staying in D \ B 1 - δ (0), hence crosses one of these 2 m ε rectangles from side to side). Also, there exists k ∈ { 1 , . . . , k δ } (specifically, k = i q j ) such that E n,q j,k occurs.

Step 3. Conclusion. Note that the construction of Γ ′ n using the counterflow lines of Ψ n combined with the fact that counterflow lines do not cross flow lines of the same field implies that ℒ cannot cross η n,q j,k a.s. Therefore, we obtain that ℒ has to contain all the points of ∂ D ∩ η n,q j,k . Note that ℒ has to surround B 1 - δ (0), otherwise the points of ∂ D ∩ η n,q j,k will be hit at least twice by ℒ (see Figure 8), which is impossible by [She09, Theorem 5.4]. In particular ℒ is the origin-containing loop of Γ ′ n and does not meet B 1 - δ (0). Since { K : K ∩ B 1 - δ (0) = ∅} is closed for the Hausdorff topology and, by (3.2), the origin-containing loop of Γ ′ n converges in law to that of a CLE 4 , the Portmanteau theorem bounds the lim sup n of the probability of the above event by the probability that the origin-containing loop of a CLE 4 does not meet B 1 - δ (0), which tends to zero as δ → 0 (that loop being a.s. at positive distance from ∂ D ). Thus one can choose δ small enough (which is also admissible above) for the lemma to hold. □

Proof of Lemma 5.34. Fix ε, p ∈ (0 , 1). By Lemma 5.35, there exist δ &gt; 0 and n 0 ∈ N depending only on ε and p such that the following holds for all n ≥ n 0 . With probability at least 1 - (1 - p ) / 100, we have that there is no loop ℒ of Γ ′ n with diameter at least ε such that ℒ ⊆ D \ B 1 - δ (0). Let F n denote that event.

̸

Next, Lemma 5.20 implies that there exists n 1 ≥ n 0 depending only on p and δ , such that the following holds for all n ≥ n 1 . With probability at least 1 - (1 - p ) / 100, we have the following. For all ℒ ∈ Γ ′ n such that ℒ ∩ ∂ D = ∅ , we have that ℒ ⊆ D \ B 1 - δ (0). Let G n denote that event.

Fix n ≥ n 1 and suppose that F n ∩ G n occurs; then there is no loop in Γ ′ n intersecting ∂ D with diameter at least ε . Moreover, note that P [ F n ∩ G n ] ≥ p . This ends the proof of the lemma. □

5.4.3. Proof of Proposition 5.2. Finally, we are ready to prove Proposition 5.2. It will follow from Lemmas 5.36 and 5.37 below combined with Proposition 5.3.

Lemma 5.36. Fix z ∈ D and 0 &lt; s &lt; t such that A s,t ( z ) ⊆ D . The probability of the event that there are loops in Γ n crossing A s,t ( z ) and that some loop of Γ ′ n crossing A s,t ( z ) does not surround any loop of Γ n crossing A s,t ( z ) tends to 0 as n →∞ .

Proof. Suppose that we are working on the event that there are loops in Γ n crossing A s,t ( z ). These loops divide the annulus A s,t ( z ) into a set of conformal rectangles. By Proposition 5.4, as n →∞ , these conformal rectangles converge in distribution to those formed by the crossings of A s,t ( z ) by a CLE 4 Γ 4 , which implies in particular that their moduli are stochastically bounded away from 0 and ∞ .

Let ℒ ′ be a loop of Γ ′ n crossing A s,t ( z ) and ℐ one of its crossing segments. Either ℐ meets one of the loops of Γ n intersecting ∂A s,t ( z ), or it avoids all of them, hence is contained in one of the conformal rectangles and crosses it between its top and bottom boundaries. In the latter case, by Lemma 5.29 applied in a rectangle R j (which depends on n since the conformal rectangles are defined via Γ n ) , ℐ is a segment of a loop of a CLE κ ′ n in R j .

By Lemma 5.19, the probability that a CLE κ ′ n loop in such a conformal rectangle crosses between the top and bottom boundaries without intersecting the left or right boundaries (which are formed by the crossings of Γ n ) tends to 0 as n →∞ (we apply Lemma 5.19 in R j , using the convergence of the rectangles R j as n →∞ and of their moduli given by Proposition 5.4, together with a union bound over the number of rectangles, which is tight).

Therefore, off an event whose probability tends to 0 as n → ∞ , any loop in Γ ′ n crossing A s,t ( z ) must intersect one of the loops of Γ n meeting ∂A s,t ( z ), hence surround it by Lemma 5.30. As ℒ ′ crosses A s,t ( z ), that loop cannot be an excursion loop of A s,t ( z ) unless ℒ ′ also surrounds a crossing loop; in either case ℒ ′ surrounds a loop of Γ n crossing A s,t ( z ), which completes the proof. □

Lemma 5.37. Fix ε, p ∈ (0 , 1) . Then, there exist ˜ ε ∈ (0 , 1) and n 0 ∈ N depending only on ε and p , such that the following holds for all n ≥ n 0 . With probability at least p , every loop in Γ ′ n with diameter at least ε surrounds a loop in Γ n with diameter at least ˜ ε .

Proof. Combining Lemmas 5.34 and 5.35, we obtain that there exist δ ∈ (0 , ε/ 100) , n 0 ∈ N depending only on ε and p such that the following is true for all n ≥ n 0 . With probability at least 1 - (1 - p ) / 100, we have that for all ℒ ∈ Γ ′ n such that diam( ℒ ) ≥ ε , there exists x ∈ ℒ ∩ B 1 - δ (0). Let A n denote that event.

Suppose that A n occurs and let ℒ ∈ Γ ′ n be such that diam( ℒ ) ≥ ε . Then, there exists z ∈ B 1 - δ (0) such that ℒ crosses the annulus A δ/ 4 ,δ ( z ). In particular, there exists z ∈ ( δ 100 Z 2 ) ∩ B 1 - δ (0) such that ℒ crosses A δ/ 3 ,δ/ 2 ( z ). Moreover, using the Brownian loop soup construction of CLE κ for κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4] (see [SW12]), we obtain that the following is true. There exists s ∈ ( δ/ 4 , δ/ 2) such that the following holds for all n ≥ n 0 . With probability at least 1 - (1 - p ) / 100, we have that for all z ∈ ( δ 100 Z 2 ) ∩ B 1 - δ (0), there exists ℒ ∈ Γ n such that ℒ crosses A s,δ/ 2 ( z ) or such that ℒ encircles A s,δ/ 2 ( z ) (in the increasing loop-soup coupling, a CLE κ 1 loop crossing the annulus is surrounded by a CLE κ n loop, which therefore crosses or encircles it). We denote that event by B n .

Note that P [ A n ∩ B n ] ≥ 1 - (1 - p ) / 50 for all n ≥ n 0 . Fix n ≥ n 0 and suppose that A n ∩ B n occurs. Then, for all ℒ ′ ∈ Γ ′ n with diam( ℒ ′ ) ≥ ε , there exists z ∈ ( δ 100 Z 2 ) ∩ B 1 - δ (0) and ℒ ∈ Γ n such that both ℒ ′ and ℒ cross A s,δ/ 2 ( z ) (since on the event that ℒ encircles A s,δ/ 2 ( z ), the loop ℒ ′ cannot cross A s,δ/ 2 ( z ) in the coupling because it would have to intersect ℒ , and hence surround it by Lemma 5.30, or else lie inside ℒ , which is impossible since the loops of Γ ′ n are sampled inside the false loops of the BCLE ⟲ κ ( - κ/ 2)'s, which are disjoint from the interiors of the true ones). Note that diam( ℒ ) ≥ δ/ 2 - s &gt; 0. Therefore, by Lemma 5.36 applied to the finitely many annuli A s,δ/ 2 ( z ), z ∈ ( δ 100 Z 2 ) ∩ B 1 - δ (0) (with a union bound and n 0 enlarged so that the total exceptional probability is at most (1 - p ) / 100), the loop ℒ ′ surrounds a loop of Γ n crossing A s,δ/ 2 ( z ), which completes the proof of the lemma. □

Proof of Proposition 5.2. Fix ε ∈ (0 , 1) and let p = 1 - ε/ 2. By Lemma 5.37, there exist ˜ ε ∈ (0 , 1) and n 0 ∈ N depending only on ε and p such that the following holds for all n ≥ n 0 . With probability at least p , every loop in Γ ′ n with diameter at least ε surrounds a loop in Γ n with diameter at least ˜ ε . Furthermore, by Proposition 5.3 applied to ˜ ε , there exists δ &gt; 0 such that for all n ∈ N , with probability at least p , every loop in Γ n with diameter at least ˜ ε surrounds a Euclidean ball of radius δ .

Let E n be the event that every loop in Γ ′ n with diameter at least ε surrounds a loop in Γ n with diameter at least ˜ ε , and let F n be the event that every loop in Γ n with diameter at least ˜ ε surrounds a Euclidean ball of radius δ . Then, for all n ≥ n 0 , a union bound gives P [ E n ∩ F n ] ≥ 1 - P [ E c n ] - P [ F c n ] ≥ 1 - 2(1 - p ) = 1 - ε .

Suppose that E n ∩ F n occurs. Then, any loop in Γ ′ n with diameter at least ε surrounds a loop in Γ n with diameter at least ˜ ε , which in turn surrounds a Euclidean ball of radius δ . Therefore, on E n ∩ F n , any loop in Γ ′ n with diameter at least ε surrounds a Euclidean ball of radius δ . To complete the proof, it suffices to note that for each fixed n &lt; n 0 , Γ ′ n is a non-nested CLE κ ′ n , which has finitely many loops of diameter at least ε , each of which bounds an open domain and therefore surrounds a Euclidean ball. Hence, for each such n there is δ n &gt; 0 such that with probability at least 1 - ε the loops of Γ ′ n of diameter at least ε all surround a Euclidean ball of radius δ n ; replacing δ by δ ∧ min n&lt;n 0 δ n gives the claim for all n . This implies the statement of the proposition. □

## 6. On the distance between two segments

Now, let us pick up the notation used in Sections 1 to 4, with the sequence κ n ↓ 4. This section aims to prove the following lemma which states that one can find two disjoint segments that are close to each other for the CLE κ n graph distance.

Lemma 6.1. For all ε &gt; 0 , there exist two disjoint segments α and β of ∂ D such that for all n large enough,

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

More precisely, there is M &gt; 0 such that for all M ′ ≥ M and n large, if one considers a CLE κ n in [0 , M ′ ] × [0 , 1] , then CLE κ n graph distance between the top and bottom sides of [0 , M ′ ] × [0 , 1] is less than ε a κ n with probability at least 1 - ε ; the statement follows by taking for α , β the images of these sides under a conformal map onto D .

Throughout this section we use the following form of the Markov property of CLE κ , κ ∈ [4 , 8) [She09, GMQ21]: for a deterministic closed set A ⊆ D so that A ∪ D is connected, conditionally on the loops meeting A , the remaining loops form independent CLE κ 's in the components of the complement of the closure of the union of A and the domains encircled by those loops.

Let us start by a simple remark.

Remark 6.2 . Let α and β be two disjoint segments of ∂ D . If α ′ ⊂ α is another segment, then D κ n ( α, β ) ≤ D κ n ( α ′ , β ). Indeed, any path of loops of Γ κ n from α ′ to β is a path of loops from α to β .

The proof of Lemma 6.1 relies on the following key steps:

- (i) We first show in Lemma 6.3 that the CLE κ n graph distance between two macroscopic segments on the boundary of the unit disk is bounded from below by a small constant times a κ n with high probability.
- (ii) We then prove in Lemma 6.5 that, with positive probability, the distance from ∂ D to ℒ κ n (0) can be arbitrarily small, and the shortest path achieving this distance can avoid a given line segment (such as [0 , 1]). This relies on the fact that ℒ (0) is macroscopically close to ∂ D with positive probability, combined with the lower bound from the previous lemma to force the shortest path to avoid the segment.

- (iii) Next, in Corollary 6.6 we show that by cutting the unit disk along the line segment from the previous step and applying a conformal map, for every ε &gt; 0 there exist L, p &gt; 0 such that, for all n large enough, the CLE κ n graph distance between the top and bottom sides of [0 , L ] × [0 , 1] is at most ε a κ n with probability at least p ('long' meaning that the extremal length of the left-right crossings is large).
- (iv) Finally, to complete the proof of Lemma 6.1, on the event that ℒ (0) is close to ∂ D , we construct consecutive disjoint, long conformal rectangles connecting ∂ D to ℒ κ n (0). By the domain Markov property, the CLE κ n distances across these rectangles are conditionally independent. Applying Corollary 6.6 to each rectangle and using independence allows us to deduce that the distance across one of these rectangles is small with probability at least 1 - ε . We then conclude using the fact that, conditionally on the loops outside the region between the outermost cuts, the restriction of the CLE κ n to this region is a CLE κ n there, whose top-to-bottom distance is therefore not affected by the conditioning.

Lemma 6.3. For all ε &gt; 0 , there exist δ, c &gt; 0 such that if α = { e i θ : θ ∈ [0 , δ ] } and β = { e i θ : θ ∈ [ π, π + δ ] } , then, for all n ≥ 1 large enough,

By Skorokhod's representation theorem, we may assume that (4.1) and the convergence of Lemma 5.1 with K = [0 , 1] hold a.s. Let F n (resp. F ) be the closure of the union of [0 , 1] and the domains encircled by the loops of Γ κ n (resp. Γ) meeting [0 , 1], so that F n → F and the components of D \ F n are simply connected. Fix z ∈ D Q \ F and let R n be the component of D \ F n containing z (well defined for n large). With probability 1 - o (1), R n is a conformal rectangle with bottom boundary B n ⊆ ∂ D , top boundary T n ⊆ ℒ κ n (0), and left and right boundaries in loops of Γ κ n \ { ℒ κ n (0) } .

By the Hausdorff convergence of F n , we see that the probability that a planar Brownian motion started from z stopped when it exits R n hits B n (resp. T n ) is a.s. bounded from below by a positive random variable, and the same is true for the left and right sides of the rectangle. In other words, if we let φ n : R n → D be the unique conformal mapping such that φ n ( z ) = 0 and φ ′ n ( z ) &gt; 0, the lengths of the segments α n def = φ n ( B n ) and β n def = φ n ( T n ) are bounded from below by a positive random variable, and the same is true for the images of the left and right sides. Note that conditionally on F n (and on the event that z / ∈ F n ), the loop ensemble ( ψ n ◦ φ n )(Γ κ n | R n ) has the same law as a CLE κ n in D . Thus, on the above event, the conditional law given F n of D κ n ( α, β ) stochastically dominates that of D κ n ( ∂ D , ℒ κ n (0)) by Remark 6.2. Finally, a - 1 κ n D κ n ( ∂ D , ℒ κ n (0)) converges in law to D ( ∂ D , ℒ (0)) &gt; 0 by (4.1), so P [ D κ n ( ∂ D , ℒ κ n (0)) ≥ c a κ n ] ≥ 1 - ε for some c &gt; 0 and n large, which ends the proof (with 2 ε in place of ε ). □

The following lemma controls the loops of a CLE 4 in a thin annular region (see [SWW17] for CLE 4 in doubly connected domains).

Lemma 6.4. For all η &gt; 0 , there exists δ &gt; 0 such that, for every simply connected domain U ⊆ D whose complement contains B 1 - δ (0) and every CLE 4 Γ U in U , with probability at least 1 - η all the loops of Γ U have diameter at most η . The same is true for doubly a connected domain U ⊆ D whose bounded complementary component contains B 1 - δ (0) .

Proof. By the Brownian loop soup construction, Γ U consists of the outer boundaries of the outermost clusters of a Brownian loop soup in U with intensity c = 1. By the restriction property, this soup consists of the loops contained in U of a loop soup S in D with c = 1, so its clusters lie in clusters of S . By [SW12, Theorem 1.6], the clusters of S of diameter at least η 0 def = min( η, 1) lie inside the finitely many CLE 4 loops of diameter at least η 0 , which do not meet ∂ D ; hence the probability p δ that such a cluster meets {| z | ≥ 1 - δ } tends to 0 as δ → 0.

In the case where U is doubly connected, we reason as follows. By [SWW17, Section 3], Γ U consists of the outer boundaries of the outermost clusters of a Brownian loop soup in U with intensity c = 1, conditioned on the event G U that no cluster surrounds the bounded complementary component of U . By the restriction property, this soup consists of the loops contained in U of a loop soup S in D with c = 1, so its clusters lie in clusters of S . By [SW12, Theorem 1.6], the clusters of S of diameter at least η 0 def = min( η, 1) lie inside the finitely many CLE 4 loops of diameter at least η 0 , which do not meet ∂ D ; hence the probability p δ that such a cluster meets {| z | ≥ 1 - δ } tends to 0 as δ → 0. On the complementary event, all the clusters of the soup in U ⊆ {| z | &gt; 1 - δ } have diameter less than η 0 , so G U holds (a cluster surrounding the hole would have diameter at least 2(1 - δ )) and all the loops of Γ U have diameter at most η . Hence the probability that some loop of Γ U has diameter larger than η is at most p δ / (1 - p δ ) ≤ η for δ small, uniformly in U . □
