From the above lemma, we deduce that two long segments can be close with positive probability.

Corollary 6.6. For all ε &gt; 0 , there exist L &gt; 0 , p &gt; 0 , and n 0 ∈ N such that for each n ≥ n 0 , a CLE κ n in [0 , L ] × [0 , 1] has graph distance between its top and bottom sides less than ε a κ n with probability at least p (a statement about each fixed n separately).

## 7. Checking the axioms

In this section, we prove that the subsequential limit D satisfies the axioms listed in Lemma 1.5. Together with Propositions 3.1 and 4.1, this proves Theorem 1.3.

7.1. Axiom (I) (weak geodesic metric). Axiom (I), (i) (connectedness of balls) is satisfied since by (4.7), ℬ t ( ℒ ) is the Hausdorff limit of ℬ κ n a κn t ( ℒ κ n ) (for fixed t , see Section 4.3), which is connected (each loop at graph distance k touches one at distance k - 1), and by Lemma 4.7, we have that ℬ t ( ℒ ) is the closure of the union of the regions encircled by the loops of Γ at D distance at most t from ℒ . For a segment α ⊆ ∂ D , the same argument gives that ℬ t ( α ) is connected.

Furthermore, Axiom (I), (ii) (right-continuity and left limits of balls) follows from (4.7), which was obtained using Lemma 4.6, and from Lemma 4.8 for the left limits (the balls being nondecreasing in t ).

Axiom (I), (iv) (geodesic sets) stems from Proposition 4.1: ˜ K ℒ ( x ) , ℒ ( y ) is a Hausdorff limit of connected sets containing both loops, and its inclusion in K ℒ ( x ) , ℒ ( y ) follows from the convergence of the balls and Proposition 7.2 below.

We postpone the proof of Axiom (I), (iii) to the end of the section.

Not only does the above proposition prove Axiom (I), (iii), but it also shows that the distances and balls from segments defined via the subsequential limit satisfy the definition given in Axiom (I). Indeed, by taking t 1 = 0, we get D ( α, ℒ ( x )) = inf { t ≥ 0 : ℬ t ( ℒ ( x )) ∩ α = ∅} .

Before proving Proposition 7.2, let us state a useful lemma on conformal rectangles in the unit disk.

Lemma 7.3. For all l, δ &gt; 0 , there exists ε 0 &gt; 0 depending only on l and δ such that the following holds for all ε ∈ (0 , ε 0 ) . Let R ⊂ D be a conformal rectangle. Let L &gt; 0 be the unique real number such that the conformal rectangle R can be conformally mapped to (0 , L ) × (0 , 1) (so that the top, bottom, left and right sides of R are mapped to the corresponding sides of (0 , L ) × (0 , 1) ). Let z ∈ D and assume that there exists some connected component ˜ R of R ∩ ( B δ ( z ) \ B ε ( z )) such that ˜ R is a conformal rectangle whose left (resp. right) boundary is a subarc of ∂B δ ( z ) (resp. ∂B ε ( z ) ) and its top (resp. bottom) boundary is a subarc of the top (resp. bottom) boundary of R , and ∂ ˜ R does not intersect the left and right boundaries of R . Then L ≥ l .

Proof. Step 1. Restriction to an annular component. Let ˜ R be a connected component of R ∩ ( B δ ( z ) \ B ε ( z )) satisfying the properties in the statement of the lemma. Note that there exists a closed subarc I of ∂B √ εδ ( z ) such that I crosses ˜ R between the top and bottom boundaries of ˜ R , and such that I intersects ∂ ˜ R only at its endpoints (the circle separates the left and right boundaries of ˜ R , so one of its crosscuts in ˜ R does).

- Step 2. A Beurling estimate. Fix w ∈ I ∩ ˜ R . Then, in order for a planar Brownian motion ˜ B starting from w to exit R for the first time either on the left or right boundaries of R , it must exit ˜ R for the first time either on the left or right boundaries of ˜ R , i.e., at some point on ∂B δ ( z ) ∪ ∂B ε ( z ). Note that dist( w,∂ ˜ R ) ≤ 2 √ εδ and that in order for ˜ B to exit ˜ R for the first time on the left boundary of ˜ R , it must traverse distance at least δ - √ δε ≥ ( √ δ - √ ε )dist( w,∂ ˜ R ) / (2 √ ε ) . Thus [Law05, Proposition 3.79] implies that this occurs with probability at most ˜ Cε 1 / 4 ( √ δ - √ ε ) - 1 / 2 for some universal constant ˜ C ∈ (0 , ∞ ).

Similarly, in order for ˜ B to exit ˜ R for the first time on the right boundary of ˜ R , it must make a crossing between the inner and outer boundaries of the annulus B √ εδ ( z ) \ B ε ( z ) without intersecting the top and bottom boundaries of ˜ R (which we recall that they both cross B √ εδ ( z ) \ B ε ( z )). It follows from [Law05, Corollary 3.78] that by possibly taking ˜ C to be larger (in a universal way), we have that the probability that ˜ B exits ˜ R for the first time on the right boundary of ˜ R is at most ˜ Cε 1 / 4 δ - 1 / 4 . Combining with the previous paragraph, we obtain that there exists a constant C = C ( δ ) ∈ (0 , ∞ ) depending only on δ such that the following holds for all w ∈ I ∩ ˜ R and all ε ∈ (0 , δ/ 2). The probability that a planar Brownian motion starting from w exits R for the first time either on its left or right boundaries is at most Cε 1 / 4 .

Step 3. Conclusion. Let ϕ be the conformal transformation mapping R onto (0 , L ) × (0 , 1) such that the top (resp. bottom) boundary of R is mapped onto (0 , L ) ×{ 1 } (resp. (0 , L ) ×{ 0 } ) and the left (resp. right) boundary of R is mapped onto { 0 } × (0 , 1) (resp. { L } × (0 , 1)). If ( L/ 2) + i(1 / 2) ∈ ϕ ( I ), then [Law05, Proposition 3.69] combined with the previous paragraph imply that if we take ε ∈ (0 , δ/ 2) sufficiently small (in a way that depends only on δ and l ), we have that L ≥ l . If ( L/ 2) + i(1 / 2) is not in ϕ ( I ), then ϕ ( I ) disconnects ( L/ 2) + i(1 / 2) either from the left or the right boundary of (0 , L ) × (0 , 1). Without loss of generality, we can assume that the latter holds since the exact same argument works in the former case. Then, by the Markov property of planar Brownian motion, we have that the probability that a planar Brownian motion B exits (0 , L ) × (0 , 1) for the first time on { L } × (0 , 1) is at most

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

But Step 2 implies that the latter quantity is at most Cε 1 / 4 and so using again [Law05, Proposition 3.69], we obtain that L ≥ l (if we take ε ∈ (0 , δ/ 2) sufficiently small depending only on δ and l ). This completes the proof of the lemma. □

We are now in position to prove Proposition 7.2.

Case 3: For infinitely many n , we have ℬ κ n a κn τ κn ε ( ℒ κ n ( x )) ∩ ∂ D = ∅ and ℬ κ n a κn t 2 ( ℒ κ n ( y )) ∩ ∂ D = ∅ . We do exactly the same proof as in Case 2, exchanging the roles of ( x, t 1 ) and ( y, t 2 ) ((7.3) being symmetric in these pairs).

Case 4: For all n large enough, we have ℬ κ n a κn τ κn ε ( ℒ κ n ( x )) ∩ ∂ D = ∅ and ℬ κ n a κn t 2 ( ℒ κ n ( y )) ∩ ∂ D = ∅ . This case is similar except that we draw a polygonal path P 1 from x to y and a polygonal path P 2 from x (or y ) to ∂ D .

These four cases are exhaustive (if none of Cases 1-3 occurs, Case 4 does for all n large).

In all the above cases, we get that D ( ℒ ( x ) , ℒ ( y )) ≤ t 1 + t 2 , thus finishing the proof of (7.3). When one of the loops (or both) is replaced by a segment of ∂ D , the same proof works. □
